# Hex-N: a silicon neural cell that learns (design document, 20 Oct 2026)

**Status in one line:** a working cell model and a Verilog prototype, a training method that works in simulation, and first size measurements. Nothing has been routed or run on a card yet. This is a separate project on its own branch (`Hex-N`); it is not meant to be merged into `main`.

This document is written for Alan (the author, not a programmer): ideas first, numbers where they were measured, and a plain line on what is not known.

## 1. What it is, and how it relates to UniCell

Hex-N is an attempt to build the silicon version of a nerve cell, and then a substrate made of many of them.

It shares its roots with Imago UniCell: "topology is computation", cells that are wired to their neighbours point to point, no central processor, and the habit of building from small proven pieces. It differs in what a cell is. A UniCell compute cell is a small register file with NOR gates, and its programme is a wiring. A Hex-N cell is closer to a neuron: it adds up what arrives, compares the total with a threshold, and fires a graded burst. Its programme is also a pattern, but the pattern is trained and not written.

| | UniCell | Hex-N |
|---|---|---|
| Cell | register file plus NOR gates | six-faced summing cell with lanes |
| Programme | a wiring, written | a pattern of settings, trained |
| Result | deterministic, known in a fixed number of clocks | deterministic, produced by the trained pattern |
| Timing | clocked dataflow | windows of two phases (forward, then feedback) |

Both are deterministic. Hex-N has no true randomness at run time; any random choice in training comes from a seeded circuit that can be replayed exactly.

## 2. The cell as it exists

A cell has six faces. Each face has a number of **lanes** (4 in the prototype; the original specification had 16, the first bring-up scoped 8). A lane is one wire whose direction is shared in time: it never carries flow both ways at once.

Per lane the settings are: active or not, a direction code, and a gate code. Per face there is a threshold. One shared value (the bias) raises every threshold.

**Direction codes:** receive only, send only, receive-then-send, send-then-receive.

**Gate codes** (applied on the sending side):
- ALL-THREE ("AND"): fire only if input arrived, the threshold was reached and feedback agreed.
- CHOOSE ("OR"): of several equal paths, the lowest-numbered one fires.
- STEER ("XOR"): steer to an even or odd lane depending on whether input arrived.
- NEGATIVE ("NOT"): a receiving lane subtracts instead of adds.

**One window.** A window has a forward phase (flow goes forward and is summed) and a feedback phase (flow comes back and is summed separately). At the end of the window the face works out: did the total reach the threshold; by how much (capped at 6); did any input arrive; did the feedback agree. In the next window a lane that passes its gate sends a burst of 1 + margin clocks, so stronger input produces a stronger signal downstream. That is the graded output.

**Where the cell's memory is today.** The only memory is those four results, held for one window. Everything else clears at each phase. The shared 16-bit bias is a fixed setting, not a memory.

Files: `rtl/hexn_cell_v1.v` (Verilog), `tests/test_hexn_cell_v1.py` (an independent cycle-accurate Python model; the RTL matches it on 60 random configurations). The model rests on seven assumptions, marked A1 to A7 in the RTL and listed in `walkthrough-v0.2.md`; they are guesses made to build the prototype, not decisions, and only Alan can settle them.

## 2a. Finding (20 Oct, measured): the faces of a cell do not talk to each other

In the prototype each face works alone: its output depends only on what that same face received, on its own threshold and on the shared bias. Changing the input on faces 0 to 2 never changed the output on faces 3 to 5 (0 of 100 random cells). Information moves between cells only along the wires, from a face to the matching face of a neighbour; inside a cell it does not move at all. So today a Hex-N cell is six independent face-neurons that happen to share a bias, not one neuron with six faces of dendrites.

This may or may not be intended. If it is, a recognition task needs a layout where each face sees its own inputs and outputs and a network is built by wiring faces together. If it is not, the cell needs a mixing step (for example one shared accumulator, or a crossbar between faces), which is a design change that would raise its size. Decision for Alan.

## 3. How it learns

The method, as Alan specified it:

1. **Train in the VM.** The pattern (which lanes, directions, gates, thresholds) is found by search in simulation.
2. **Freeze it.** The trained pattern becomes the programmed cell, as a UniCell wiring would.
3. **Optionally mutate in silicon.** If the cell can change its own settings and score the result, it can retrain in place.

The search used is not gradient training (the settings are discrete). It is: start from a working cell, change 1 to 3 settings at a time, keep the change if the score is not worse, otherwise undo it. A random starting point is replaced by the best of 100 random trials.

**What the score is.** For every lane and every window, the number of clocks the lane was high is compared with the goal's. 1,000 means identical bursts, 0 means no overlap. Forward and feedback phases are scored separately and combined (half and half works best).

**On-chip learning needs only small parts** (tested in the VM, not yet in RTL): a 32-bit random-number circuit (replayable), a spare copy of the last good settings, and one teacher reward. The reward needs about **4 bits** per trial; with 3 bits it is a little worse, and below that it fails. A teacher that only says "better or not" (1 bit) works as well as the 4-bit reward when the "not worse" rule is used.

## 4. What has been measured (all in the VM unless stated)

Single cell, 30 seeds, 1,500 tries each. "Random guessing" is the best of the same number of random settings. Scores are 0 to 1,000.

| Step | Mutated score | Beat random guessing |
|---|---|---|
| Exact-bit score, keep-or-undo (v1) | about 600 | about half of seeds |
| Graded burst score (v2) | 627 | 18 of 30 |
| Plus warm start and 1 to 3 changes per try (v5) | 808 to 828 (three runs) | 27 to 29 of 30 |

Run-to-run noise in a mean of 30 is roughly 30 to 60 points; earlier conclusions drawn from small differences were not reliable.

**Networks: no valid result yet.** Two earlier network tests (`hexn_network_v7.py`, `hexn_network_v8.py`, a line of 1 to 8 cells) reported that learning faded from 4 cells. That is **withdrawn**: in the cell as built, information never crosses from one face to another inside a cell (section 2a), so in those line networks the external input never reached the cell whose output was scored (checked: its output did not change at all when the input changed, in 0 of 40 networks at 2, 4 and 8 cells). The 1-cell rows were valid. The network question is open and needs a proper test (section 2a).

**Memory of older input.** When the right output depends on input from earlier windows, a plain cell loses score steadily: delay 0 scores 833, delay 1 641, delay 2 524, delay 3 443. Adding a small writable memory (a shift register of "input arrived", with trained taps that the AND and XOR gates can read) lifts delay 4 from 390 (none) to 475 (4 bits), 563 (8 bits) and 480 (16 bits). The same memory costs score when it is not needed (delay 0: 761 without, 615 to 677 with). A live-input AND was tried and cannot change anything in this cell as built, so it is not a result. The 8-bit memory could share the existing 16-bit latch (8 memory, 8 bias), which needs no extra storage. More sweep results (other delays) will be added here when they are complete.

**Size (synthesis estimate, one cell, 6-bit accumulator, no place and route, settings excluded):**

| Lanes per face | Gowin LUTs (Tang Nano 20K) | Xilinx 7-series LUTs (Kintex) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 450 | 359 |
| 2 | 600 | 407 |
| 3 | 968 | 539 |
| 4 | 1,280 | 677 |
| 8 | 2,719 | 1,186 |

Flip-flops stay at 117. The settings (82 bits at 1 lane, 172 at 4, 292 at 8) are inputs in this prototype and would add storage. Tool: `tools/hexn_size_v1.sh`, yosys 0.33. The learning-versus-lanes result is still running and will be added.

## 4a. Pattern recognition and the hex cluster (20 Oct 2026, VM only; all numbers from the files named)

**Cell v2 (Alan's choice):** all six faces feed one decision (one shared forward total, one feedback total, one threshold); the earlier cell (v1) was six independent faces. Model `tools/hexn_cell_v2_model.py`, RTL `rtl/hexn_cell_v2.v` (checked against the model by `tests/test_hexn_cell_v2.py`), size tables `tools/hexn_size_v2.sh`.

**Task:** four classes of 12 on/off wires, noise = chance each wire is flipped, chance level 0.25, "template rule" ceiling about 0.90 at noise 0.15 (0.72 at 0.25). A single detector cell per class reaches about 0.68; one cluster of 7 cells (radius 1) with one shared reward reached 0.45 (random best 0.42, `hexn_cluster_v15.py`).

**What was tried, one change at a time on the same seeds (v18 to v20).** Accept rule fixed in advance (`tools/hexn_ladder_compare_v1.py`): the mean paired difference must be at least 2 standard errors and the new arm must win on more seeds than it loses.

| Change | Result | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| Local reward per output group instead of one reward (v16) | 0.25 at 600 tries, 0.29 at 2,000 (global reward: 0.45) | worse |
| Outputs that never vary with the input get no credit (v18 reward) | 0.199 (25 seeds) | baseline for the ladder |
| Threshold offset, clamped, driven by firing (v18, M=2, run 3) | 0.227 vs 0.199 | no difference shown |
| Grow from one-in-one-out cells, with stuck-lane pruning (v17) | 0.00 to 0.26 depending on arm | no help |
| Feedback also pushes the offset (first form) | identical on all 25 seeds | VOID: its vote could never oppose the firing vote |
| Feedback agreement cancels the push (v19 fbagree) | 0.170 vs 0.227 | leans worse, not accepted |
| Offset decay | 0.247 vs 0.227 | no difference shown |
| **Readout score: reward also counts "my group fires strictly more than the others"** | **0.312 vs 0.199 (+0.112, 3.6x its error, 19 wins 5 losses)** | **accepted** |
| Aimed mutations (shared output cells) | 0.309 vs 0.312 | no difference shown |
| Per-face weights 1 to 3, trained, fixed at run time | 0.329 vs 0.312 | no difference shown |
| **Each output group served by its own cell (`--ownout`)** | **0.385 vs 0.315 over 50 seeds (+0.070, 3.35x, 31 wins 16 losses); the last 25 seeds alone also pass** | **accepted** |
| Aimed mutations again, now with one cell per output group | 0.366 vs 0.376 (25 seeds) | no difference shown |
| Longer search, 2,000 tries instead of 600 (15 seeds only) | 0.418 vs 0.366 (+0.052, 1.6x its error, 8 wins 4 losses) | no difference shown; leans better, would need more seeds |
| Graded readout term: how much the right group leads in burst clocks (Alan's idea, 21 Oct) | 0.348 vs 0.376 (25 seeds; 6 wins 11 losses) | no difference shown; leans worse |
| Panic shake: after 60 tries without strict improvement, change 8 to 12 settings at once (Alan's "keep the openness") | 0.378 vs 0.376 (25 seeds) | no difference shown; with 600 tries the shake fires only a few times, so a longer search would be a fairer test |
| **Decision-table cell** (`hexn_cell_v3_model.py`, `hexn_cluster_v21.py`; 64-entry table of output levels indexed by which faces had input; 768 bits per cell; Alan's idea, 21 Oct) | 0.421 vs 0.385 over 50 seeds (+0.036, 1.4x its error, 29 wins 21 losses); sd 0.107 vs 0.134; worst 0.22 vs 0.01; seeds under 0.20: 0 vs 3; best 0.73 | no difference shown on the mean. The spread looks better (0 failures in 50 against 3) but on the second 25 seeds alone the summing cell also had none, so the consistency gain is not established. Not yet sized in logic. |

Vs the hand-set bank (0.859): T=8 -0.017 (ratio -1.36, 10 wins 14 losses), T=4 -0.023 (ratio -2.24), T=12 -0.023 (ratio -2.37), T=16 -0.064 (ratio -3.43). Spread: T=8 sd 0.067, worst 0.69, best 0.94; T=12 sd 0.053, worst 0.72; all 25 seeds at 0.50 or more at every T. Lanes given the right sign / left off / wrong sign (of 48): T=4 42.8 / 3.8 / 1.3; T=8 38.7 / 8.6 / 0.6; T=12 33.6 / 14.2 / 0.1; T=16 25.6 / 22.3 / 0.0 | REAL (accept): counting beats every searched bank by a wide margin and sits within about 0.02 of the hand-set ceiling for T=4 to 12 (a partial 15-seed look had suggested an exact tie with the hand-set bank at T=8; the full 25 seeds show 0.017 below, not a real difference at T=8, a small real shortfall at T=4 and T=12). Too high a commit threshold leaves too many lanes off (T=16). Still VM only, one task, labelled samples, thresholds still searched |
| Robustness of counting (hexn_tally_v2.py; commit threshold T = half the samples per class, fixed in advance; compared with the best searched bank, hexn_bank_v5 --lanes weight --thr-max 63 --thr-steps 1,10, 1,000 tries, and the hand-set bank hexn_oracle_v2.py; 25 seeds each; test always 200 fresh samples at the same noise) | HIGHER NOISE 0.25, 16 samples/class (template rule 0.708): counting 0.651, searched 0.530 (+0.121, ratio +7.66, 23 wins 1 loss), hand-set 0.652 (-0.001, tie); counting worst seed 0.52, all 25 at 0.50 or more vs 18 for the search. 8 SAMPLES/CLASS, noise 0.15 (rule 0.893): counting 0.793, searched 0.666 (+0.127, ratio +8.64), hand-set 0.818 (-0.025, ratio -1.85). 4 SAMPLES/CLASS: counting 0.723, searched 0.574 (+0.150, ratio +6.79, 22 wins 3 losses), hand-set 0.736 (-0.013, no difference). 2 SAMPLES/CLASS: counting 0.528, searched 0.440 (+0.089, ratio +3.40, 18 wins 7 losses), hand-set 0.672 (-0.143, ratio -7.58, 0 wins 24 losses); lanes right sign / off / wrong of 48: 37.7 / 3.8 / 6.4 (T=1 commits too eagerly) | REAL (accept) in all four conditions against the searched bank, with the gap about the same (+0.09 to +0.15). Counting stays close to the hand-set ceiling down to 4 samples per class (within 0.025) and falls clearly short only at 2 samples per class, where the counters commit lanes on too little evidence (6.4 wrong). Counting also never has a seed under 0.32 in any condition. Counting-trained detectors degrade gracefully with less data and more noise; the searched bank degrades faster |
| Counted thresholds (hexn_tally_v3.py --thr-rule): no search left. HIST = each detector run alone on the training samples, threshold at the forward-total value that best separates own class from the rest (balanced accuracy, two count tables); MID = midpoint of the mean own-class total and the mean other-class total. Lane weights counted as before (T=8, 16 samples/class, noise 0.15, 25 seeds) | HIST 0.816 vs searched thresholds 0.842 (-0.025, ratio -2.63, 6 wins 17 losses); MID 0.824 vs 0.842 (-0.018, ratio -1.38, 8 wins 15 losses). Both vs the best searched bank (0.739): HIST +0.078 (ratio +5.57, 21 wins 3 losses), MID +0.085 (ratio +6.48, 22 wins 3 losses). Vs the hand-set bank (0.859): HIST -0.042, MID -0.035. Spread is tighter than with searched thresholds: HIST sd 0.044, worst 0.70, best 0.89; MID sd 0.046, worst 0.74, best 0.93; all 25 seeds at 0.50 or more | A fully counted detector (counted lane weights and a counted threshold, no search anywhere) costs about 0.02 against searched thresholds (real for HIST, not shown for MID) and still beats every searched bank by about 0.08; MID is the simplest (two running averages per detector) and is not distinguishable from HIST |
| Fully counted detector (counted lane weights, T = half the samples per class, MID counted threshold, no search anywhere) in the tougher conditions (25 seeds each) | NOISE 0.25, 16/class: 0.618 vs searched bank 0.530 (+0.088, ratio +5.76, 22 wins 3 losses), vs counted weights with searched thresholds 0.651 (-0.033, ratio -2.69), vs hand-set 0.652 (-0.034); worst seed 0.52, all 25 at 0.50 or more. 8/class: 0.794 vs searched bank 0.666 (+0.128, ratio +7.07), vs searched thresholds 0.793 (+0.001, tie), vs hand-set 0.818 (-0.024). 4/class: 0.738 vs searched bank 0.574 (+0.165, ratio +7.89, 23 wins 2 losses), vs searched thresholds 0.723 (+0.015, no difference), vs hand-set 0.736 (+0.002, tie). 2/class: 0.614 vs searched bank 0.440 (+0.174, ratio +6.41, 23 wins 2 losses), vs searched thresholds 0.528 (+0.086, ratio +5.72, 21 wins 4 losses), vs hand-set 0.672 (-0.058, ratio -3.12) | REAL (accept) against the searched bank in every condition (+0.09 to +0.17). The counted threshold costs about 0.03 at higher noise, nothing at 8 and 4 samples per class, and BEATS the searched threshold at 2 samples per class (a threshold searched on 8 training samples overfits; the counted midpoint does not). A fully counted detector is the best design found so far and degrades gracefully with less data and more noise |
| Harder tasks for the fully counted detector (hexn_tally_v4.py; 12 wires, 16 samples/class, noise 0.15, 25 seeds; patterns at least D wires apart; template rule = nearest true pattern) | K=4, D=4: 0.824 (template rule 0.901, chance 0.25); K=4, D=2: 0.775 (0.903); K=8, D=4: 0.738 (0.836, chance 0.125); K=8, D=2: 0.638 (0.806); K=16, D=4: 0.522 (0.727, chance 0.062); K=16, D=2: 0.447 (0.709). Worst seeds 0.74, 0.56, 0.60, 0.41, 0.39, 0.25; seeds at 0.50 or more: 25, 25, 25, 24, 18, 9 of 25. Share of the template rule's accuracy kept: 0.91, 0.86, 0.88, 0.79, 0.72, 0.63 | The counted detector degrades gracefully with more classes and overlapping patterns, staying 5 to 8 times above chance at K=16; it keeps 79 to 91% of the data's own ceiling up to 8 classes and 63 to 72% at 16 classes, so it starts to struggle when classes are many and close together. Only 12 input wires are used, which itself limits how separable 16 classes can be. Not compared with a searched bank here (already clearly worse at K=4) |
| SUPERSEDED, see the corrected size rows below (the numbers here came from yosys chparam, which gave a smaller circuit than the one simulated): SIZE of the counting (training side) in logic (rtl/hexn_tally_v1.v, checked against the Python model: RTL matches in 16 of 16 checks, 4 seeds x 4 detectors, tools/hexn_tally_rtl_check_v1.sh; synthesis only, native yosys, tools/hexn_size_tally_v1.sh): one detector, 12 lanes, 6-bit saturating counters per lane, weight compares, score popcount, two 12-bit score accumulators and two 8-bit sample counters | Gowin (Tang Nano 20K family): 430 LUTs, 112 flip-flops, 357 carry/ALU cells; Xilinx 7-series: 236 LUTs, 112 flip-flops, 36 carry cells. 24 lanes: Gowin 881 LUTs, 184 FFs; Xilinx 440 LUTs, 184 FFs. 5-bit counters: Gowin 410 LUTs, 100 FFs. For reference the existing run-time cell v2 with 4 lanes per face (24 lanes) is 1,236 LUTs, 22 FFs, 257 carry on Gowin and 589 LUTs on Xilinx | the training side of a counted detector is about a third of a run-time cell in LUTs and about five times its flip-flops. This is a deliberately naive fully parallel version (12 saturating adders at once); the counters could be updated one lane at a time (about 12 clocks per sample) with one shared adder, which would cut the logic to a small fraction of this: not measured yet. The threshold division is not included (a host or a small divider forms the midpoint from the two accumulators and counts) |
| WIDER INPUT: 18 wires (lanes 0..17; output on lane 20) instead of 12 (hexn_tally_v5.py; 16 samples/class, noise 0.15, 25 seeds; template rule 0.957 at K=4, 0.92 at K=8, 0.87 at K=16). With the cell as built (accumulator 6 bits, forward total saturates at 63, about 6 agreeing lanes) | counted accuracy (K=4 / 8 / 16): 0.851 / 0.670 / 0.421 at pattern gap 4; 0.851 / 0.648 / 0.446 at gap 2 (at 18 wires the random patterns of K=4 are almost always already 4 apart, so gaps 4 and 2 are the same task there). Against the same task at 12 wires: K=4 gap 2 +0.076 (ratio +3.36); K=8 gap 4 -0.068 (ratio -2.86); K=16 gap 4 -0.102 (ratio -3.69) | MORE WIRES MAKE IT WORSE with the 6-bit accumulator for 8 and 16 classes: the forward total saturates, so the detector can no longer tell a good match from a very good one |
| Same, accumulator widened to 8 bits (total saturates at 255; aw 8; only this changes; 25 seeds) | K=4: 0.865 (gap 4 and 2); K=8: 0.798 (gap 4), 0.778 (gap 2); K=16: 0.638 (gap 4), 0.623 (gap 2); worst seeds 0.68, 0.64, 0.60, 0.50, 0.50; seeds at 0.50 or more 25 of 25 in every cell. Against aw 6 at 18 wires: K=8 +0.128 and +0.130 (ratios +6.31, +7.20, 24 wins 0 losses), K=16 +0.217 and +0.176 (ratios +11.21, +8.82, 25 wins 0 losses), K=4 +0.014 (not significant). Against the 12-wire result: K=4 +0.041 (gap 4), +0.090 (gap 2); K=8 +0.060, +0.140; K=16 +0.115, +0.176 (all ratios above +2.5; K=16 gap 2: 25 wins 0 losses). Share of the template rule's accuracy kept: 0.90, 0.87, 0.85, 0.73, 0.72 | REAL (accept): widening the input to 18 wires pays off once the accumulator is wide enough (8 bits); the gain grows with the number of classes (K=16 from 0.52 to 0.64 at gap 4, from 0.45 to 0.62 at gap 2) and every seed reaches 0.50. The cell's 6-bit accumulator is the limit on usable input width, a finding about the cell, to be raised separately |
| CORRECTED SIZE of the counted detector, training side, 12/18/24 input lanes (synthesis only, native yosys; parameters set by editing the defaults of a temporary copy, tools/hexn_size_tally_v3.sh; both RTL versions match the Python model in 16 of 16 simulation checks): PARALLEL (rtl/hexn_tally_v1.v) | Gowin: 544 / 703 / 884 LUT4, 112 / 148 / 184 flip-flops, 429 / 621 / 813 ALU cells; Xilinx 7-series: 256 / 394 / 504 LUTs, 112 / 148 / 184 FFs, 36 / 48 / 60 carry. SERIAL (rtl/hexn_tally_serial_v1.v, one lane per clock, one shared adder): Gowin 571 / 850 / 1,109 LUT4, 188 / 304 / 322 FFs, 113 / 114 / 114 ALU; Xilinx 208 / 291 / 326 LUTs, 188 / 304 / 322 FFs, 23 / 24 / 24 carry. Reference, existing run-time cell v2 at 24 lanes: Gowin 1,236 LUT4 + 257 ALU, 22 FFs; Xilinx 589 LUTs | the serial version is NOT smaller on Gowin (571 vs 544 LUT4 at 12 lanes, and more flip-flops) and only about 20% smaller on Xilinx at 12 lanes (208 vs 256): the lane multiplexers and the held weights cost as much as the adders save. The per-lane counters (6 bits each) are the real cost: 72 flip-flops at 12 lanes. A saving would need the counters in block RAM, not measured. Counting Gowin ALU cells as logic, the parallel 12-lane training side is about 970 cells, about two thirds of a 24-lane run-time cell. My earlier claim that serial updating would cut the logic to a small fraction was wrong |
| ACCUMULATOR WIDTH against INPUT WIDTH (hexn_tally_v5.py --wires --aw; fixed task: 8 classes, patterns at least 2 apart, 16 samples/class, noise 0.15, 25 seeds; template rule 0.562 / 0.806 / 0.918 / 0.944 at 6 / 12 / 18 / 20 wires) | mean accuracy by accumulator bits 5 / 6 / 7 / 8 / 9 / 10: 6 wires 0.564 / 0.599 / 0.599 / 0.599 / 0.599 / 0.599; 12 wires 0.442 / 0.638 / 0.662 / 0.662 / 0.662 / 0.662; 18 wires 0.410 / 0.648 / 0.778 / 0.778 / 0.778 / 0.778; 20 wires 0.396 / 0.622 / 0.811 / 0.812 / 0.812 / 0.812. Paired gains of each extra bit: 6 wires 5 to 6 bits +0.034 (ratio +3.74) then exactly nothing; 12 wires 5 to 6 +0.196 (+8.40), 6 to 7 +0.024 (+3.70, 14 wins 2 losses), then exactly nothing; 18 wires 5 to 6 +0.239 (+9.07), 6 to 7 +0.129 (+7.24, 24 wins 0 losses), then nothing; 20 wires 5 to 6 +0.227 (+8.44), 6 to 7 +0.188 (+10.19, 25 wins 0 losses), 7 to 8 +0.001 (not significant), then nothing | SATURATION POINT FOUND, and it moves with the input width: 6 bits for 6 wires, 7 bits for 12, 18 and 20 wires (wider gains nothing: results identical to the last seed). Too narrow an accumulator costs more the wider the input (5 bits: 0.564 at 6 wires, 0.396 at 20). So the width has to grow with the wires, but slowly: about one bit per doubling, 7 bits covering 12 to 20 wires; 24 or more wires will probably need 8 bits (not testable here: the cell has only 24 lanes and the output takes one). Detector accuracy at the plateau: 0.599 / 0.662 / 0.778 / 0.811, i.e. 107%, 82%, 85%, 86% of the template rule (the 6-wire task is almost at its own ceiling) |
| COST of the accumulator width in the run-time cell (tools/hexn_size_aw_v1.sh; hexn_cell_v2, 24 lanes, synthesis only, native yosys, defaults edited in a temporary copy; the chparam method gives the same for AW 6: 1,236 vs 1,238 LUT4 on Gowin, 589 = 589 on Xilinx) | accumulator 5 / 6 / 7 / 8 / 9 bits: Gowin LUT4 1,230 / 1,238 / 1,245 / 1,256 / 1,263, flip-flops 20 / 22 / 24 / 26 / 28, ALU 253 / 257 / 261 / 265 / 269; Xilinx LUTs 582 / 589 / 598 / 608 / 616 | each extra accumulator bit costs about 7 LUTs and 2 flip-flops, about 0.6% of the cell: going from 6 to 7 bits (needed for 12 to 20 wires) is almost free. The cell's logic as written; lane settings and thresholds storage are not in these numbers |
| MINIMAL DETECTOR as the base unit (Alan, 21 Oct: use the counted detector as the base unit). rtl/hexn_det_v1.v and hexn_det_v2.v: only what the detector needs (receive-only lanes each off/+1/-1, one forward total, one threshold, one output burst; no feedback total, no choose chain, no direction/code decode). Checked clock by clock against the full hexn_cell_v2 (rtl/tb_hexn_det_v1.v: 24,000 clocks, 400 random trials; same output every clock at 12 wires/6 bits, 12/7 and 20/7). v1 counts + and - lanes with two separate counters; v2 uses one signed sum (tools/hexn_size_det_v1.sh, _v2.sh; synthesis only, native yosys, defaults edited in a temporary copy) | v1, 12/18/20 wires: Gowin 435 / 937 / 1,011 LUT4 + 105 / 183 / 203 ALU; Xilinx 165 / 341 / 394 LUTs. v2: Gowin 150 / 224 / 268 LUT4 + 35 / 39 / 39 ALU, 13-14 flip-flops; Xilinx 79 / 117 / 121 LUTs, 9-11 carry. Full cell for reference: Gowin 1,238-1,245 LUT4 + 257-261 ALU; Xilinx 589-598 | the detector is about 5 to 8 times smaller than the full cell on Gowin (v2, counting ALU cells as logic: about 185 / 263 / 307 against about 1,500) and 5 times on Xilinx. The way the lanes are counted matters a lot: v1 was 3 to 4 times bigger than v2 for the same behaviour. Not included: lane settings (2 bits per lane plus the threshold, 30 to 46 bits per detector) and the training side. Estimate at 80% of a Tang Nano 20K (about 16,600 slots): roughly 50 to 90 detectors depending on width, against 11 full cells; the Kintex number depends on the part and has not been checked |
| TRAINING BANK, one shared training unit for a whole bank of minimal detectors (Alan, 21 Oct: the training bank is the next essential part). rtl/hexn_trainbank_v2.v (+ hexn_sdiv_v1.v divider, hexn_cfgbank_v2.v the detectors' settings as a shift chain); v1 (rtl/hexn_trainbank_v1.v) kept: it held the weights itself and was much bigger. Serial: the K*NB lane counters sit in ONE memory read and written a lane at a time, weights come straight from the counters, one divider forms every midpoint threshold. Commands CLEAR, COUNT (each training sample), SETW, ACCUM (each sample again), FINISH. Check: tools/hexn_trainbank_stim_v1.py + rtl/tb_hexn_trainbank_v2.v + tools/hexn_trainbank_check_v2.sh: weights and thresholds of every detector identical to the Python counted-detector training (tally_v5 rule, same samples and order) in 10 of 10 cases (K 4/8/16, 12/18/20 wires, 6/7-bit accumulator, 16 and 8 samples per class); a deliberately damaged copy gives different output, so the check can fail. Sizing: tools/hexn_size_train_v2.sh | Trainer alone, K = 4 / 8 / 16 detectors at 12 wires, then K = 16 at 20 wires: Gowin 470 / 825 / 1,383 / 1,567 LUT4 + 218 / 234 / 246 / 259 ALU, 188 / 290 / 496 / 528 flip-flops; Xilinx 234 / 306 / 399 / 456 LUTs. The trainer barely grows with the number of detectors (the counter memory is one block). Detector settings as a shift chain: no logic, only flip-flops (120 / 240 / 480 / 752). With the address-decoded first version of the settings block they cost 473 / 1,516 / 3,064 / 5,061 LUT4 on Gowin: that was my wrapper, not the detectors. Training time for the whole procedure: 5,740 clocks (K 4, 12 wires, 8 samples per class) up to 290,268 clocks (K 16, 20 wires, 16 per class), i.e. about 11 ms at 27 MHz if the board clock is 27 MHz (not checked) | a complete 16-class, 20-wire bank on the Tang Nano 20K is about 16 x 307 (detectors) + 1,826 (trainer, counting ALU cells as logic) = about 6,700 slots, about 41% of the 80% mark (33% of the chip), about 1,500 flip-flops; roughly 30 more detectors, or 6 full cells, would still fit under 80%. On the Kintex 480T (about 298,600 LUT6 from memory, not checked; the '480' is Xilinx logic cells) the same bank is about 2,400 LUTs, under 1%. NOT yet done: one end-to-end simulation of trained RTL detectors classifying test samples (the chain is: trainer = Python, minimal detector = full cell, full cell = model; each link checked, the whole chain not run in one piece); the settings shift chain relies on the trainer writing each setting once in order; synthesis only (no place and route, no timing) |
| WHOLE BANK, END TO END, and FAN-OUT / ROUTING COST (Alan, 21 Oct: look at the fan-out wiring, it counts to the costings; then an end-to-end run). New: rtl/hexn_bank_v1.v (K minimal detectors + their settings + the shared training unit + run-time controller with the window timing + one burst counter per detector + answer = unique strict maximum above zero), rtl/hexn_bank_pnr_top_v1.v (serial-load wrapper so it fits the board's pins), tools/hexn_bank_stim_v1.py, rtl/tb_hexn_bank_v1.v, tools/hexn_bank_check_v1.sh (end to end), tools/hexn_pnr_v1.sh (place and route), tools/hexn_fanout_est_v1.py (hex-grid estimate) | END TO END: the RTL trains itself from the training samples with the trainer commands and then classifies the test samples; every test sample (answer and all K burst counts) is identical to the Python model in 8 of 8 setups (K 4/8/16, 12/18/20 wires, 6/7-bit accumulator, 200 to 800 test samples each). RTL accuracy equals the Python accuracy in each: K=4 12 wires 0.740 and 0.800, K=8 12 wires 0.765, K=4 18 wires 0.920, K=4 20 wires 0.880, K=8 20 wires 0.833, K=16 12 wires 0.603, K=16 20 wires 0.623 (single seeds, not averages). A deliberately damaged copy (counting in every window instead of the last) gives 380 differing lines, so the check can fail. PLACED AND ROUTED for the Tang Nano 20K (device GW2AR-LV18QN88PC8/I7, native yosys then YoWASP nextpnr-himbaechel-gowin, target 27 MHz, numbers from the post-route report; the whole bank incl. trainer, settings, controller and a ~30-flip-flop load register): K=4/12 wires: LUT4 1,080 + ALU 528 + 437 FF, 184 MHz; K=8/12: 1,951 + 786 + 730 FF, 158 MHz; K=16/12: 3,888 + 1,330 + 1,315 FF, 165 MHz; K=8/20 wires: 2,606 + 860 + 912 FF, 163 MHz; K=16/20: 5,143 + 1,422 + 1,657 FF, 148 MHz. Counting LUT4 + ALU as slots: 1,608 / 2,737 / 5,218 / 3,466 / 6,565 of 20,736 = 7.8% / 13.2% / 25.2% / 16.7% / 31.7% of the chip (the 80% mark is 16,589). Routed connections: 5,585 / 9,397 / 18,494 / 11,959 / 23,327. Each input wire drives 9 to 18 loads at K=4, 19 to 45 at K=8, 37 to 100 at K=16 (12 or 20 wires); the largest fan-outs in the design are the reset and control nets (about 1,300 to 1,700 loads), not the data wires | on the FPGA the fan-out costs nothing measurable beyond the logic: the placed-and-routed K=16/20-wire bank is 6,565 slots against my earlier add-up of about 6,740, it meets timing at 5 times the board clock, and the input wires (up to 100 loads each) route fine. Fan-out is routing, which the chip has in plenty. The earlier estimate that a full 16-class bank takes about a third of the chip is confirmed by routing. HEX-GRID FAN-OUT (tools/hexn_fanout_est_v1.py, a COUNT with stated assumptions, not a layout or a measurement): a face carries 4 wires, so NB wires need ceil(NB/4) input faces plus one output face: at most 20 wires per detector. Distributing the groups of 4 wires through relays that each serve up to 5 detectors needs at least ceil(NB/4) x ceil(K/5) relays: 12 for K=16 at 12 wires, 20 for K=16 at 20 wires. If each relay is a small repeater (about 4 flip-flops) that adds about 1.6% to the detectors; if each relay is a full run-time cell (about 1,500 slots) it adds about 600% (30,000 slots for K=16 at 20 wires: it would NOT fit the Tang). So on a hex grid the relays should be repeaters, not full cells, which goes against the 'compose from existing cells' rule and is Alan's decision. Whether a lattice layout with every detector next to the right relays exists is NOT checked. The earlier limited-fan-out result (each detector seeing 8 of 12 wires lost nothing) suggests fewer connections may be enough, but it was measured on the older summing-cell bank at 12 wires and not repeated for the counted detector |
| 12-WIRE LATTICE DESIGN (Alan, 21 Oct: start with 12 wires, see how it fits, get it onto the Tang, run tests). The pattern: a cell LISTENS on faces 0, 2, 4 (one of each line direction) and keeps 1, 3, 5 free; one bridge (a 4-wire register) on every listened edge = 3 bridges per cell, each edge counted once; groups A, B, C of 4 wires run along the straight lines of the three directions, first bridge of each line fed from the input bus. Files: tools/hexn_lattice_v1.py (layout and propagation check), tools/hexn_lattice_gen_v1.py (writes rtl/gen/hexn_lattice_<shape>_v1.v), rtl/hexn_bank_v2.v (per-detector inputs and a settle wait), tools/hexn_lattice_check_v1.sh | LAYOUT (checked on a hex lattice in axial coordinates, five patches): every cell hears all three groups, no gaps in any line, 3 bridges per cell (12 / 21 / 24 / 48 / 57 bridges for 4 / 7 / 8 / 16 / 19 cells), longest line 2 / 3 / 4 / 4 / 5 hops, input-bus feeds per group 2-3 / 3 / 2-5 / 4-7 / 5 lines. A clocked propagation test shows every cell sees the right 12 wires after the longest line's number of clocks. RTL END TO END on the lattice (bridges are real registers, the bank waits for them to settle): every test sample (answer and all burst counts) identical to the Python model in 7 of 7 cases (4, 4, 7, 7, 8, 16 and 19 detectors; accuracy 0.74, 0.80, 0.71, 0.75, 0.76, 0.48, 0.42: with only 12 wires, 16 and 19 classes at minimum distance 4 are hard). BOARD VERSION: rtl/hexn_tang_top_v1.v with a serial command link (rtl/hexn_uart_v1.v, 115200 baud through the BL616; commands CLEAR, COUNT, SETW, ACCUM, FINISH, RUN), simulated at byte level with the testbench acting as the host (tools/hexn_tang_sim_v1.sh: 350 of 350 test samples identical to Python for 7 detectors), host script tools/hexn_tang_host_v1.py (seeds x noise levels, compares board with model; its --selftest checks only the script's own framing against a Python stand-in), loader wrapper tools/hexn_tang_run_v1.sh, build tools/hexn_tang_build_v1.sh. BITSTREAMS (placed, routed and packed with Apycula, pins from docs/man/tang-nano-20k.cst): 4 detectors 1,903 slots (9.2% of the chip), 575 FF, 176 MHz; 7 detectors 2,985 slots (14.4%), 833 FF, 115 MHz; 16 detectors 5,598 slots (27.0%), 1,479 FF, 163 MHz; 19 detectors: routing failed for placement seed 1 on one net at about 30% use, but placement seeds 2 and 3 both route: 7,413 slots (35.7%), 2,058 FF, 108 and 101 MHz (so a tool placement issue, not capacity; build with SEED=2) | the 12-wire pattern fits and works in simulation, down to the register. The bridges cost 12 flip-flops per detector (3 x 4); my earlier count of 1.6% assumed relays shared by up to 5 detectors, the lattice pattern gives every cell its own three, so the figure is about 12 FF per detector on top of the roughly 185 slots of the detector (on an FPGA a flip-flop shares a slot with a LUT, so the slot count above already contains them). NOT DONE: nothing here has run on the board (the UART receive pin 70 and the whole serial path are unverified on hardware; the repository's reference pin list is itself unchecked against the schematic); the detectors' outputs reach the winner logic by plain wires in this RTL (the output collection path on the free faces is not laid out); only 12 wires (18 or 20 need more lanes per face or more groups per direction); on the FPGA the lattice is only a wiring pattern, the real test of the hex-grid idea is silicon |
| ON THE TANG NANO 20K, hex7 (7 detectors, 12 wires), serial command link (22 Oct 2026, first silicon run; rtl/hexn_tang_top_v1..v5.v, tools/hexn_tang_build_v*.sh, tools/hexn_tang_host_v1.py; placed and routed 7 detectors: about 3,989 of 20,736 slots, 90 MHz against the 27 MHz board clock; builds for rhombus4, par16 and hex19 also place and route, not yet loaded) | The board loads, the serial link works (every command acknowledged, runs return an answer). Simulation (RTL and gate-level netlist of the earlier design) matched the Python model on every sample, but the first board runs did NOT: identical-to-model on 216 of 5,250 test samples, board accuracy 0.06 to 0.24. | **RESOLVED 22 Oct 2026, see the next two rows.** The first board runs disagreed with simulation; the cause was found by reading each training stage back from the board. |
| Board findings so far (seed 0 and seeds 0-4, hex7; commands DUMP 0x07, DUMPSUMS 0x08, DIVTEST 0x09, PROBE 0x0A, PEEK 0x0B) | (1) the trained weights of all 14 detector weight masks read back from the board equal the model exactly; (2) the thresholds do not (board 32 to 47, model 5 to 40); (3) the running sums of the threshold pass are wrong on the board (sumP about 650 to 961 against 140 to 816, sumN 3,733 to 5,187 against 30 to 1,240) and the board's thresholds follow correctly from its own sums; (4) the serial divider is correct (24 of 24 tests); (5) PROBE, one training sample at a time: 132 of 140 (sample, detector) results differ with the signed-sum accumulation and 134 of 140 with the two-unsigned-counters variant, board nets are large and positive (4 to 6) where the model's are negative or small, so the signed arithmetic is NOT the cause; (6) PEEK (all 84 trainer counters read back after COUNT, SETW, ACCUM and FINISH): 0 of 84 differ at every point, in the RAM build and in the flip-flop (noram) build, so counting, the counter memory and SETW are correct on silicon; (7) a build with the counter memory as plain flip-flops behaves differently (accuracy about 0.74 to 0.79 at noise 0.05) but not like the model either. | The fault is in how the ACCUM pass uses the correct counters (the weight it forms, the input bits or threshold it sees). Next: compare the synthesized netlist of the current design against the RTL on the PROBE test (running), then fix and re-run on the board. This row is the state at the first diagnosis; the fix is in the rows below. |
| BOARD FAULT FOUND AND FIXED (22 Oct 2026; same design files, only the synthesis option differs) | Further read-backs added to the board top (ECHO 0x0C: sample bits, label, T as the trainer holds them; CAP 0x0D: for each of the 84 weight writes the counter value seen, the weight formed; host options --echo, --wstage, --cap, --probe, --peek). Findings in order: sample bits, label and T on the board were right; the board's totals agreed with a recomputation from its own bits and weights; in the builds with the serial or the one-clock ACCUM the trained weights differed from the model in a way that changed from build to build (+1 weights all missing in one build, mixed wrong weights after the comparisons were rewritten as plain logic, all right in the CAP build) although RTL and the synthesized netlist simulated correctly; in the CAP build the weights were right but 36 and 62 of 140 probe results still differed (totals off by 1; negative totals showing the maximum forward total 63). | Building the SAME design with `synth_gowin -noalu` (no carry-chain cells: adders and comparators as plain logic; tools/hexn_tang_build_v10.sh, NOALU=1; bitstream hexn_tang10_hex7_acc0_noalu.fs): probe 0 of 140 differ; seeds 0 to 4 at test noise 0.05 / 0.15 / 0.25: the board is identical to the Python model on 5,250 of 5,250 test samples, board accuracy equal to model accuracy in every row (for example seed 0: 0.946 / 0.737 / 0.494; seed 3: 0.931 / 0.754 / 0.494). Cost of the option: 7,312 LUT4 slots (35.3% of 20,736) against 5,934 slots with carry-chain cells, and the tools report 70.7 MHz against 108 to 128 MHz, board clock 27 MHz. **Conclusion supported by the measurements: with the carry-chain (ALU) cells the placed design gave wrong arithmetic on this board; without them it is exact.** NOT established: whether the fault lies in the tool flow (yosys / nextpnr-himbaechel-gowin / Apycula packing) or in the chip, or which cell in the design triggered it. |
| 19 DETECTORS ON THE BOARD (22 Oct 2026; hex19 lattice, 19 detectors x 12 wires, built with NOALU=1, placement seed 2, bitstream hexn_tang10_hex19_seed2_noalu.fs; simulation first: identical to the Python model on 950 of 950 test samples) | Placed and routed: 11,631 LUT4 slots (56.1% of 20,736), 2,439 flip-flops, no carry-chain cells, tools report 42.2 MHz (placement seed 3: 38.4 MHz) against the 27 MHz board clock. These are the design's limit for 19 classes on 12 wires (see the 16-class rows: 12 / 18 / 20 wires gave 0.554 / 0.638 / 0.676 in the VM), not a board fault. Timing margin at 42 MHz against 27 MHz is thinner than for hex7 (108 to 128 MHz). Host speed: the Python model setup for 19 classes takes 1 to 7 minutes per seed (search for patterns at least 4 apart); tools/hexn_bank_model_v2.py keeps finished models in tools/model_cache/ (seeds 0 to 4, K = 7 and 19 are committed); the board's own work is fast. |
