BIST at NC=128 on this core v2 build not yet re-run. |

| BIST ON THE 128-CELL CORE V2 BUILD, KINTEX CARD (23 Oct; build_hex19_nc128_c2, tools/hexn_card_bist_v1.py --cells 19 --wires 18 --aw 7) | run straight after the cells test, same load | BIST PASS: all 8 sets, every stage ok (342 counters, 342 weights, 19 sums, 19 thresholds, 76 answers and bursts per set) and 24 of 24 divider tests; battery coverage as before (at_T 33, below_T 32, at_negT 30, above_negT 23). Alan noted it ran faster than the earlier BIST; no timing was recorded, so the cause is not established. |

| CARD RAM FOR THE MEMORY BLOCK: WHAT THE KINTEX CARD OFFERS (23 Oct; read from Alan's ypcb-00338-1p1 notes and designs/uberddr3/memtest.v, nothing run by us) | board README: two 72-bit DDR3 channels (64 data + 8 ECC), 2 GiB each, 9x MT41K256M8 per channel; designs/uberddr3 (openXC7 + UberDDR3, GPL, source kept outside every repo at ~/fpga/src/UberDDR3, commit 79d8fd3, cloned on the OptiPlex 23 Oct) runs both channels at DDR3-667, 5.04 GB/s each, 0 errors; internal block RAM works under openXC7 (designs/bram, bramwidths) | Interface (from memtest.v): Wishbone, pipelined, stall and ack lines, acks return in order, 25-bit word address, 576-bit word (one burst of 8 x 72 bits; 512 data + 64 ECC bits assumed, not confirmed usable as data). UberDDR3's page gives simulation figures only: about 10 ns per request sequential, 47 ns random at a 100 MHz controller clock; NO read latency is stated anywhere and none has been measured on the card. Alan's earlier "8 Mbit" was a misremembered size; the card has gigabits, so RAM capacity is not the limit for a hashed tier. Python models (tools/hexn_ram_tier_v1.py, v2) assumed 8 Mbit and latencies 10/30 cycles: those are guesses and must be re-run with measured numbers. Planned next step: a small latency test (random single reads, min/avg/max cycles, then three pipelined reads) in a new folder in the Hex-N tree, simulated first. Also open: the notes say about one placement in four leaves one DDR3 lane uncalibrated and that Alan's fixes resolve it (which is current is unchecked); a DDR3 build must not run while the 255-cell ladder build is using the machine's RAM. Alan also wants the UniCell design revisited later to see how it fits this card with a proper RAM interface. |

| 255-CELL BUILD (CORE V2) ON THE KINTEX CARD, THE TOP OF THE LADDER (23 Oct; build_hex19_nc255_c2, hex19 shape, 255 cells = the protocol maximum, started 21:26, bitstream 22:35) | build: synth 1054 s, pnr 2914 s, frames 162 s (about 69 minutes in all, on the OptiPlex with THREADS=4); nextpnr max frequency 60.33 and 65.98 MHz on br.clk (PASS at 50), br.drck 775 and 787 MHz; real silicon clock still unmeasured; bitstream 18,735,108 bytes (same size as every build, it covers the whole chip) | CARD RESULT: cells test (tools/hexn_tang_cells_v1.py via hexn_card_run_v1.py, example_random19_script_v1.txt, --ncell 255 --episodes 40): trained settings 0 of 19 detectors differ; 240 frames, frames with any cell differing 0; differing cell results 0 of 61200; cell outputs that fired (rules) 30301; CELLS TEST PASS. BIST right after (--cells 19 --wires 18 --aw 7): BIST PASS, all 8 sets every stage ok, 24 of 24 divider tests. RUNCELLN replies here are 2*255+19 = 529 bytes, the largest frame so far. Ladder on silicon: hex7, hex19 NC19, NC64, NC128 (core v2) and NC255 (core v2) all pass. Going past 255 cells or 19 detectors needs a protocol change or several banks. |

| RAM LATENCY PROBE FOR THE CARD'S DDR3 (23 Oct; fpga/ypcb/hexn_ram/memlat.v, tb_memlat.v, tools/hexn_memlat_sim_v1.py; SIMULATION ONLY, not built for the card) | Reads only, same Wishbone signals as ypcb designs/uberddr3/memtest.v. Modes: single random reads (min/max/sum latency in controller clock cycles, stall cycles), pipelined random reads, pipelined sequential reads (total cycles). Not yet wired into a card build: that needs the uberddr3 prebuild.py and its USER1 register layout. | Against the stand-in slave: single reads measure exactly the slave latency (20 gives min = max = 20, sum 1000); extra latency on one address in eight shows in max and sum; stalls show in stall_sum; pipelined 100 reads at latency 20 finish in 121 cycles (about count + latency, so overlapping works in the probe); no write is ever issued. MEMLAT SIM PASS. This proves the counting only; the real latency of the card is still unmeasured. |

| RAM LATENCY PROBE: PLUGGED INTO THE UBERDDR3 BUILD PATH (23 Oct; fpga/ypcb/hexn_ram: memtest.v drop-in with the same module name and ports as ypcb designs/uberddr3/memtest.v, prebuild.py that runs ypcb's own uberddr3 prebuild unchanged, memlat.py host script; SIMULATION ONLY, not built, nothing run on the card) | Written from ypcb designs/uberddr3 prebuild.py, memtest.py and check.py as Alan pasted them. Results ride in the old memory test's USER1 words 23 to 29; mode 1/2/3 = single random / pipelined random / pipelined sequential, reads = 2^len. Controller clock assumed 83.333 MHz for the ns conversion (memtest.py's own constant), to be checked with check.py's measured figure. | tools/hexn_memlat_sim_v1.py now also runs the probe through the memtest wrapper: MEMLAT SIM PASS including wrapper cases (single 2^5 reads at latency 20 give min = max = 20, sum 640; pipelined 2^6 reads finish in about count + latency). The wrapper synthesises in yosys with no errors. memlat.py's decoding was checked on a made-up register only. Unknown until Alan shows the ypcb fpga script: how a design folder's .v files and top module are picked up. |

| OPENOCD VERSION OF ypcb jtagdr FOR THE UBERDDR3 SCRIPTS (23 Oct; fpga/ypcb/hexn_ram/jtagdr_openocd.py, run_with_openocd.py) | ypcb tools/jtagdr.py reads the USER register through xsdb (Alan has no Xilinx tools), so ypcb check.py and our memlat.py could not run on the OptiPlex. jtagdr_openocd.py gives the same calls over the OpenOCD link the Hex-N tools already use (drscan on the xc7 tap, IR 0x02, bit 0 = first bit out); run_with_openocd.py substitutes it for `jtagdr` and runs the unchanged script. | Checked only with a fake scan (a 1024-bit register split into 32 words, the control write packed as expected, gray_to_bin round trip for 0..63); NOT yet run against the card. The DDR3 latency build (designs/hexn_ram, top ypcb_00338_1p1_ddr3, BUILD=build_lat, output on /mnt/hdd/hexn_builds/ram_lat because /home had 3.5 GB free) was started by Alan at about 22:52; its prebuild printed "uberddr3 79d8fd3: channel 0, 9 byte lanes, CK phase 60, 449 XDC lines". |

| DDR3 READ LATENCY MEASURED ON THE KINTEX CARD (23 Oct; first run on silicon of the hexn_ram probe; build designs/hexn_ram = ypcb uberddr3 top ypcb_00338_1p1_ddr3 with memlat in place of memtest; channel 0, 9 byte lanes, CK phase 60; loaded with openFPGALoader; check.py and memlat.py run through run_with_openocd.py, so the OpenOCD stand-in for jtagdr works on the card) | build: synth 62 s, pnr 341 s, frames 195 s; nextpnr controller_clk 138.72 / 153.14 MHz (PASS at 50). check.py: calib_complete=1, state 23, BIST 33,554,431 reads correct and 0 wrong, 3 calibration resets all in lane 3 (then calibrated), controller clock measured 83.38 MHz (83.33 vs the 50 MHz input), ddr3 333.33 MHz | RESULT (one run, 2^10 = 1024 reads per mode, 83.333 MHz controller clock, 64-byte words, reads only): SINGLE random reads: latency from request accepted to ack min 9, mean 9.9, max 11 cycles = 108 / 118 / 132 ns; plus 649 cycles of waiting on stall over the 1024 reads (0.63 per read), so a read costs about 12.5 cycles from one request to the next including the probe's own 2. PIPELINED RANDOM: 1024 reads in 2232 cycles = 2.18 cycles (26 ns) per read = 2.45 GB/s, stall cycles 1197. PIPELINED SEQUENTIAL: 1110 cycles = 1.08 cycles per read = 4.92 GB/s (the ypcb notes say 5.04 GB/s; 1024 reads include the start-up latency), stall cycles 76. Reading: latency is about 10 cycles (about 120 ns), which is the optimistic end of the 10/30 guesses in tools/hexn_ram_tier_v1.py and v2; pipelined reads do overlap (a read every 2.2 cycles even at random addresses); random throughput is about half of sequential. NOT YET DONE: more reads (--len 15) for rare long delays such as refresh (max 11 from only 1024 reads is a small sample); the latency excludes time spent waiting on stall; the Python tier models still use the old guesses and should be re-run with these figures; cycles here are controller clock cycles (83.3 MHz), not the Hex-N core clock, which is unmeasured on silicon. |

| DDR3 LATENCY, LARGER SAMPLE, AND THE TIER MODEL RE-RUN WITH MEASURED COSTS (23 Oct; memlat.py --len 15 = 32,768 reads per mode on the card; tools/hexn_ram_tier_v3.py, clone of v2 left untouched; model only) | CARD: single random reads x32768: latency min 9 / mean 9.9 / max 11 cycles (same as 1024 reads), stall 20,346 cycles (0.62 per read), 12.5 cycles per read; pipelined random 2.22 cycles per read = 2.41 GB/s (stall 39,858); pipelined sequential 1.06 cycles per read = 5.04 GB/s (matches the ypcb notes; stall 1,901). The probe uses a fixed address seed (7), so the 1024-read run is the first 1024 reads of the 32,768-read run and the repeat of the len 10 run gave identical numbers: this is NOT independent sampling. Max latency 11 in 32,768 reads means refresh shows up as stall (waiting before the request is accepted), not as a longer latency. | MODEL with measured costs (serial 12.5 cycles per table, reads issued together 9.9 for the first plus 2.22 for each further; one bucket = one 64-byte word; hot block assumed 1 cycle, in controller-clock cycles; the printed header line still says 8 Mbit, which was Alan's misremembering, the table used is 24,576 entries and fits regardless): mean cycles per lookup, 3 tables, serial / together: no buffer 38.5 / 15.4; 8 hot slots 37.4 / 15.0 (Zipf 0.8), 33.8 / 13.6 (1.0), 22.2 / 9.2 (1.3); 32 slots 34.8 / 14.0, 27.6 / 11.2, 14.1 / 6.0; 128 slots 29.9 / 12.1, 21.1 / 8.8, 8.8 / 4.0. Accuracy unchanged (right 0.96 to 0.99, wrong 0.000). So issuing the table reads together more than halves the lookup cost, and the 8-slot buffer pays only when a few objects dominate. |

| PLACED SIZE OF THE KINTEX LADDER BUILDS (23 Oct; nextpnr utilisation from /mnt/hdd/hexn_builds/sweep_results.txt as pasted by Alan; hex19 shape, core v2 for 128 and 255) | 64 cells: SLICE_LUTX 31,428 / 597,200 (5%), SLICE_FFX 8,675 (1%), CARRY4 1,321 / 74,650 (1%), DSP48E1 24 / 1,920 (1%). 128 cells: 49,300 (8%), 13,219 (2%), 2,090 (2%), 23; the _c2 rerun of 128: 49,263, 13,223, 2,092, 24. 255 cells: SLICE_LUTX 86,459 (14%), SLICE_FFX 22,240 (3%), CARRY4 3,619 (4%), DSP48E1 24 (1%). | Per added cell about 280 to 290 LUT sites (64 to 128: 279; 128 to 255: 293) and about 70 flip-flops; the DSP blocks do not grow with the cells. The earlier yosys figure (62,847 LUT, about 21% of 298,600 LUT6) counts differently from nextpnr's 86,459 of 597,200 sites (14%); use the nextpnr figure for how much of the chip is used. Linear extrapolation only (not built): the chip would hold several times more cells by LUT count, but clock estimates already fell from 74 MHz at 128 cells to 60 to 66 MHz at 255, and routing would limit first; going past 255 cells needs a protocol change in any case. |

| SIZE OF THE RAM TIER AND BUFFER ON THE KINTEX CARD, ROUGH (23 Oct; the DDR3 figure is PLACED, from the nextpnr log of the hexn_ram latency build; the hash and buffer figures are HAND ESTIMATES, a synthesis attempt stalled in the sandbox so none were measured; nothing combined has been built) | MEASURED, nextpnr, hexn_ram build (UberDDR3 controller and 9-lane PHY, clocking, the 1024-bit USER1 debug register and the latency probe, one channel): SLICE_LUTX 15,214 / 597,200 (2%), SLICE_FFX 7,269 (1%), CARRY4 217 / 74,650, DSP48E1 0, PAD 122 / 946 (12%). ESTIMATED: checking one RAM word (8 patterns x 64 wires, closest wins) about 450 LUT; hash index and bucket handling about 1,000 LUT and 1,500 FF; hot buffer of 8 / 32 / 128 slots x 64 wires in flip-flops about 400 / 1,600 / 6,400 LUT and 512 / 2,048 / 8,192 FF (about 50 LUT to compare one 64-wire pattern, a rule of thumb; keeping +-7 counters makes the flip-flops about 4x, the compare logic about the same). | Rough total next to the 255-cell Hex-N build (86,459 LUT sites, 22,240 FF): + DDR3 15,214 + hash and 8-slot buffer about 2,000 = about 103,700 LUT sites (17%); with a 128-slot buffer about 109,700 (18%); flip-flops about 31,500 to 39,000 (5 to 7%). The DDR3 figure includes the debug register and the probe, so the controller alone is somewhat less. Not covered: timing and routing of a combined design, and one conflict to resolve first: both the Hex-N JTAG bridge and the uberddr3 debug register use BSCANE2 USER1, so a combined build needs one of them moved to another user chain (USER2 to USER4). |

| PLAN FOR THE NEXT SESSION (23 Oct late evening; Alan: the protocol change that going past 255 cells needs is the right moment to also make the changes for the RAM side; to be looked at in the morning; nothing started) | Open items gathered today | (1) Protocol v2 for the JTAG bridge: more than 255 cells (RUNCELLN reply is 2*NC+K bytes, NC is a byte in LOADCELLN) and more than 20 detectors (2 bits per detector in 5 lane bytes); decide this together with RAM-side commands. (2) Move one of the two USER1 users (Hex-N bridge, uberddr3 debug register) to another BSCANE2 user chain so one bitstream can hold both. (3) First joint design: the memory block talking to the DDR3 controller through a hash table (3 tables, a bucket = one 64-byte word, table reads issued together), hot buffer in front. (4) Re-run the Python tier models after any change to the assumed costs; add a --seed option to memlat.py for independent samples. (5) Run the odd-looks write rule (7 looks, plain) as the default on a board for sequence prediction. (6) Measure the real silicon clock of the Hex-N core. (7) Revisit how UniCell fits the card with a proper RAM interface. (8) /home is at 95%: free space there. |

| INFERENCE PLAN BEYOND SEQUENCE PREDICTION: NEAR TERM, LONG TERM AND COMBINATION (24 Oct; Alan's framing after asking how similar-but-not-identical cases could be reused and how two inputs could make a new rule; PLAN ONLY, nothing built or modelled) | Near term = the fast memory block (what is active now, recall in about one cycle); long term = the hashed table in the card DDR3 (far larger, about 10 controller cycles per read, measured 23 Oct); combination = a lookup that takes two inputs, from either tier, and returns the outcome stored for that pair (a pattern of input A wires, input B wires and outcome wires; cue with A and B). Every lookup ends as KNOWN (match within a few wires: reinforce) or NEW (no close match: store); that is the distance test the write rules already use (merge within 2 wires; merges at 3 or more wires gave wrong merges), and the false-known rate is the existing "never-stored called familiar" figure. | Ideas to test in the Python model first: (a) reuse by storing the CHANGE (state to next state) instead of the absolute next state, so a similar situation can apply the same change; (b) a two-input table, including chaining two stored rules (A leads to X, then X with B leads to Y); a combination never shown is NOT recalled, so a genuinely new rule needs an outcome or feedback (the learning-from-results stage); (c) promotion of often-used patterns from RAM into the fast block and demotion of old ones out; (d) second-order prediction (longer histories). Not built: any combination lookup, any movement between tiers. |

| TWO-INPUT TABLE AND CHAINING, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; first model of the "combination" from the inference plan; tools/hexn_combo_v1.py; mean of 10 seeds; device answers vs the Python copy of the block: 0 of 118,629 differ; NOT on a board) | A rule = one 18-wire pattern: wires 0-5 input 1, 6-11 input 2, 12-17 outcome; cue with the two inputs (low 12 wires), read the outcome from the high 6. Symbols = random 6-wire codes at least 3 wires apart (only 8 such codes exist, so the alphabet is tiny and the block's 8 slots are the limit; this studies the mechanism, not capacity). World: hidden rules g(a) = x and h(x, b) = outcome, so the true answer for (a, b) is h(g(a), b); 4 a-symbols, 2 b-symbols, so 8 pairs. DIRECT table: shown 6 of the 8 pairs. CHAIN: stores (a, none, x) and (x, b, outcome) = 8 slots, answers pairs never seen together by two lookups. | FIRST RUN HAD A LEARNER DEFECT (logged, then fixed in the same file before commit): with the old familiar test (0.75 of the known wires) a pair that differs from a stored one in a single input counts as familiar (9 of 12 wires match), so it was never stored (direct table 0.72 right at q 0.05) and unseen pairs were answered wrongly (0.12 to 0.26). FIXES: store a pair also when the block knows a similar pair but gives a different outcome; call the block familiar only at 0.85 of the known wires or more for a two-input cue. RESULT (3 looks to ask; q = noise per wire): q 0 to 0.05: DIRECT seen pairs 1.00 / 0.99 right, the 2 unseen pairs 0.00 right, 0.00 wrong, about 1.00 'unknown' (the correct 'new' answer); CHAIN 1.00 / 0.99 right on all 8 pairs and 1.00 / 0.98 on the 2 pairs the direct table never saw. q 0.10: direct seen 0.95, chain 0.90 (0.85 on the unseen pairs). q 0.15: direct seen 0.79, chain 0.63; wrong answers stay at about 0.01, the loss shows as 'unknown'. More looks to ask restore it (fam 0.85): 5 looks at q 0.15: direct 0.91, chain 0.89; 7 looks at q 0.10: 1.00 / 1.00, at q 0.15: 0.94 / 0.94, at q 0.20: 0.77 / 0.65. The familiar threshold matters: 0.75 gives wrong answers on unseen pairs, 0.92 refuses too much at q 0.10 or above (chain 0.46 right). Limits: the chain needs all 8 slots (the block is full, no room to learn anything else); a pair that was never seen is only answered if it can be built from stored rules; nothing is invented. |

| NEAR TERM AND LONG TERM WITH MOVEMENT BETWEEN THEM, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_tiers_v1.py; 2 seeds, 5,000 lookups, 4,000 stored objects, Zipf 1.0 with the popular set changing half way, 64-wire keys, 2 noisy wires per cue, hashed RAM T=3 K=11 B=4; model only, nothing on a board) | Fast block of S slots (assumed 1 cycle, least recently used leaves) in front of the hashed RAM (all three tables read together = 9.9 + 2.22 x 2 = 14.3 controller cycles, MEASURED; a RAM WRITE is ASSUMED to cost the same, not measured). Policies: promote on the first RAM hit or only on the second; an update (20 percent of hits) written to RAM at once ('through') or when the pattern leaves the fast block ('back'). Workloads: zipf; scan (the same plus bursts of 64 once-asked objects; with the 1 percent start chance about 39 percent of lookups are scan lookups, a heavy load); new (10 percent never-seen objects, stored on arrival). | Mean cycles per lookup, zipf: no fast block 18.2; 8 slots 16.3 (promote first) / 15.7 (second) / 15.3 (second, write-back); 32 slots 13.9 / 13.2 / 12.3; 128 slots 11.0 (second, through) / 9.6 (second, write-back); fast-block hit rate 0.13 to 0.18 at 8 slots, 0.30 to 0.35 at 32, 0.51 at 128. RAM writes per lookup 0.20 (through) against 0.14 (32 slots, back) and 0.11 (128 slots, back). Scan: hit rate falls to 0.08 to 0.10 at 8 slots and 0.28 at 128; promoting on the second hit helps only a little (cycles 16.7 against 16.9 at 8 slots, 14.3 against the same load with first-touch not run at 128). New: 0.43 hit at 128 slots, 12.2 cycles with write-back. Accuracy is unaffected by the tiers: right 0.97 to 0.99 of stored objects, wrong 0.00, unknown 0.01 to 0.03; never-seen objects called known (false known) 0.00 everywhere, but the patterns are random 64-wire codes, so this is optimistic for real, similar data. NOT MODELLED, and each is a real design issue: (1) the pattern content never changes, so an update is only a cost; a write-back copy leaves the RAM copy stale until the pattern leaves the fast block (harmless in a single sequential host, a hazard with concurrent access or power loss); (2) a new arrival can overwrite an older object in a full RAM bucket while a copy of that object is still in the fast block, so a later write-back must re-insert it; (3) the fast tier here is 64 wires x S slots, which is NOT the existing block (18 wires, 8 slots) and would need new hardware (about 6,400 LUT at 128 slots, an estimate); (4) many patterns per RAM word, and similarity structure in real data. |
| TIERS MODEL v2: THE TWO HAZARDS MADE REAL, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_tiers_v2.py, v1 untouched; 2 SEEDS ONLY, 5,000 lookups, 4,000 objects, 10 percent never-seen arrivals, updates on 20 percent of hits, a second reader that sees only the RAM on 5 percent of lookups; model only, nothing on a board). CORRECTION to the row above: its phrase 'against the same load with first-touch not run at 128' is garbled and is not a result; only the 8-slot scan figures (16.7 against 16.9) were compared. | Each object now has a value that really changes on an update. Copies: one in the fast block, one set in the RAM tables. Policies: through (write RAM at once); back-naive (write on leaving the fast block; if the RAM copy was overwritten the update is dropped); back-reinsert (put it back into the RAM first); back-reinsert+flush (also write every dirty pattern each 200 lookups). RAM size 'roomy' K=11 and 'tight' K=9 (a quarter of the buckets). | (1) STALE COPY: the main host never sees an old value under any policy ('stale main' = 0 in all 16 rows, the check the model was built to make). A RAM-only second reader sees an old value on 18 to 35 percent of its reads under write-back (0 under through); the number of updated-but-unwritten patterns at any moment is 7 (32 slots) to 32 (128 slots), all lost on a power failure. A flush every 200 lookups halves both (128 slots: 0.35 to 0.18 stale, 32 to 15 at risk) and costs about 0.5 cycle per lookup. The write-back saving is about 1 cycle per lookup (15.54 to 15.06 at 32 slots, 13.45 to 12.48 at 128 slots, roomy), RAM writes 0.28 to 0.21 per lookup. So write-back is cheaper but only safe if nothing else reads the RAM and power loss is tolerable; otherwise through, or write-back with a flush, costs about 0 to 0.5 cycle more. (2) OVERWRITTEN COPY: with the roomy RAM there were NO orphans (0 per 1000 lookups). With the tight RAM, 0.5 (32 slots) to 2.9 (128 slots) per 1000 lookups; back-naive then loses 0.1 to 0.8 updates per 1000 and forgets the object, back-reinsert loses none and costs the same cycles (13.81 against 13.80), so the re-insert rule is cheap and should be the rule. But the tight RAM already answers 'unknown' on 41 to 44 percent of known objects (right 0.56 to 0.59), so the orphan problem only appears when the RAM is already overfull and the real fix there is size, not policy. (3) and (4) are still open and unmodelled: the fast tier against the existing 18-wire 8-slot block, and many patterns per RAM word with similar data. The second reader is a stand-in (random objects, reads only), not a measured access pattern. |
| TIERS MODEL v3: CAN THE EXISTING 18-WIRE, 8-SLOT BLOCK BE THE FAST TIER? PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_tiers_v3.py, clone of v2, v2 untouched; 2 SEEDS ONLY; 5,000 lookups, 4,000 random 64-wire objects, 2 flipped wires per cue, write-back with re-insert, roomy RAM; model only, nothing on a board) | The fast tier looks at only WF of the 64 wires, with a distance limit TF (WF=18 stands for the existing block, which looks at 18 wires). A defect found and fixed in this model on the way: with a narrow look the fast tier can miss an object it already holds, and the RAM hit then promoted it a second time (duplicate, crash); a re-promotion now moves the held copy. Not modelled: the real block stores counters (+-7), not plain bits, and the choice of which 18 wires. | Cycles per lookup, no fast tier 19.28. 8 slots: 64 wires 17.60 (wrong 0.000); 18 wires, TF=2 17.42 (wrong 0.4 percent, never-seen objects called known 0.5 percent); 18 wires, TF=1 17.61 (wrong 0.1 percent, false known 0.1 percent); 32 wires 17.69 (wrong 0). 32 slots: 64 wires 15.17 (wrong 0); 18 wires 14.75 (wrong 1.3 percent, false known 0.7 percent); 32 wires 15.27 (wrong 0); 48 wires 15.09 (wrong 0). 128 slots: 64 wires 12.51 (wrong 0); 18 wires 11.62 but wrong 4.7 percent, false known 9.1 percent, right falls 0.98 to 0.935; 32 wires 12.58 (wrong 0.1 percent); 48 wires 12.53 (wrong 0). Reading: (a) the existing 8-slot, 18-wire block works as a fast tier at its own size, with about 0.1 to 0.4 percent wrong answers, but it only saves about 1.7 cycles per lookup of 19.3 (hit rate 11 percent); (b) its extra 'hits' above the full-width figure are the wrong ones, so they are not a gain; (c) the 18-wire look stops being safe as the slot count grows (1.3 percent wrong at 32 slots, 4.7 at 128); about 32 wires are needed for 128 slots with under 0.2 percent wrong; (d) so the useful fast tier is new, wider hardware (32 to 64 wires, 32 to 128 slots), not a reuse of the 18-wire block, with the 18-wire block still fine as a small first stage. These patterns are random; real, similar data will make the narrow look worse, which is unmodelled (issue 4 still open). |
| TWO SMALL NOISE-TOLERANT FRONT ENDS FEEDING ONE LARGER EXACT MODEL, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_pair_v1.py; 3 seeds, 1,500 queries of each kind per setting; idea from Alan; model only, nothing on a board; this is a memory-level model, not a model of the counted-detector cells) | Objects are pairs (a, b) of 64-wire codes, each half one of V=200 stored codes, N=4,000 pairs stored out of 40,000 possible. PAIR: each front end finds the nearest of its 200 codes (limit 8, a tie means unknown) and gives a clean id; the big model then looks up the id pair EXACTLY, with no distance and no noise handling. SINGLE: one 128-wire model in the hashed RAM tier (T=3, K=11, B=4, limit 16). Codes random, or clustered (each code a few flips from one of 20 centres: spread 12 and 6, the hard case). Cues carry 2 or 4 flipped wires in each half. Queries: known pairs, unseen combinations (both halves known, pair never stored: must be unknown) and fully new pairs. | Known pairs right: PAIR 1.000 in every setting (wrong 0.000, unknown 0.000 shown to 3 decimals); SINGLE 0.913 (random, 2 flips), 0.772 (random, 4), 0.749 (spread 6, 2 flips), 0.587 (spread 6, 4 flips), with up to 0.096 WRONG at spread 6. Unseen combinations: PAIR always unknown (correct); SINGLE called 47 percent of them a known pair at spread 6, 2 flips (19 percent at 4 flips), 2 percent at spread 12, 0 for random codes. New pairs: both unknown. Bits stored: SINGLE 512,000; PAIR 89,600 (25,600 for the two code books plus 64,000 for the id pairs in the big model). Cycles per lookup (read costs measured, the fast block assumed): SINGLE 14.3; PAIR with the front ends read from RAM 24.2 (front ends in parallel, then one dependent exact read), so SLOWER; PAIR with the 200 codes held in a fast compare-all block about 10.9 (1 + 9.9, assumed 1 cycle for the compare). Reading: the noise handling only has to exist where the world is noisy (the small front ends); the big model becomes an exact table, 5.7 times fewer bits here and far fewer wrong answers on similar data, but only because the objects are built from a small vocabulary of repeating halves (V=200). If the halves do not repeat (V near N) the front ends grow to the size of the single model and the advantage goes. A front end that confuses two codes kills the pair; with clustered codes the tie rule turned none of the 4,500 per setting into errors here, but codes closer than the cue noise would. Not modelled: the real Hex-N core is counted detectors, so how a 64-cell model yields a clean code id (its recall output) and how many codes it can hold is the next thing to check, on the real block. |
| TWO LOOKS AS A NOISE MEASUREMENT, FED BACK, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_noise_fb_v1.py then v2, v1 untouched; idea from Alan; 3 seeds, 1,500 known and 1,500 never-seen lookups per setting; model only, nothing on a board; the store is a plain compare-all memory of 500 random 64-wire patterns, NOT the real block (no counters, no 8-slot limit)) | Each look flips every wire independently with probability p. A = one look, fixed familiar threshold 0.75 (the current way). B = two looks, wires where they disagree made unknown, fixed threshold. C = B plus the disagreement rate turned into a noise estimate p_hat that sets the threshold and, when p_hat > 0.12, triggers a third look (majority of three). D = three looks, majority per wire (the odd-looks reference). v2 adds C2 and E, whose threshold has a floor at the chance level for the number of known wires k, 0.5 + 4.2 x 0.5 / sqrt(k); E = two looks, mask, chance-level threshold only. | v1 (B, C): detection is recovered (p=0.25: right 0.99 against 0.57 for one look) but never-seen objects were called known 28 percent (B) and 64 percent (C) of the time at p=0.25 (C 23 percent at p=0.15, B 12 percent): a DEFECT in my threshold, not a property of the idea, because dropping wires leaves fewer known wires and a chance match passes more easily. v2 (threshold from the chance level for k): E right 1.000 at p up to 0.15 and in the quiet/noisy mix, never-seen called known 0.003 to 0.005 (A 0.016 to 0.021, D 0.018 to 0.021), two looks; at p=0.25 E is 0.906 right with 0.094 unknown (A 0.565, D 0.980 at three looks with false known 0.018). C2 (noise estimate decides on a third look): 0.961 right at p=0.25, false known 0.006, 2.5 to 3 looks. Reading: nearly all the gain comes from MASKING the disagreeing wires plus a threshold set from the number of known wires (a chance-level test); the noise estimate itself adds little beyond deciding when a third look is worth taking (C2 0.961 against E 0.906 at p=0.25, no difference in the mix). Not modelled: noise that is the same in both looks (nothing is cleared), a real change between the two looks (looks like noise), similar rather than random stored patterns (discarding wires removes the wires that tell similar patterns apart, likely worse), and the real block's counters and 8 slots. The 4.2 sigma floor is sized for about 500 candidates; it must scale with the candidate count. |
| TWO-LOOK NOISE FEEDBACK ON SIMILAR STORED PATTERNS, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_noise_fb_v3.py, imports the v2 strategies, v1 and v2 untouched; 3 seeds, 1,500 queries of each kind per setting; model only, plain compare-all store, not the real block) | 500 stored 64-wire patterns in 25 families (family centre with SPREAD flipped wires; siblings differ by about 2 x spread: 21, 11, 6 wires). Queries: known; SIBLING (never stored, from a stored family); random (never stored). Strategies as v2: A one look, D three looks majority, E two looks masked with the chance-level threshold, C2 as E plus the noise estimate and a third look when p_hat > 0.12. | Known objects: masking holds up on similar patterns. E answers right 1.000 up to the second-noisiest case and 0.919 to 0.995 at the worst settings (spread 3 or 6, p=0.15), against A 0.618 to 0.895; wrong answers E 0.000 to 0.010, A up to 0.048, D 0.000 to 0.003 (D uses three looks). Random never-seen objects called known stay below 0.6 percent for E (A 0.5 to 1.8 percent). The hard case is the SIBLING: a never-seen family member is called a known object 20 to 60 percent of the time by every strategy, and E is WORSE than A there (0.43 to 0.60 against 0.27 to 0.43 at spread 3 and 6) because it compares fewer wires. The exception is C2 when the sensor is quiet: its threshold follows the measured noise, so with p=0.03 it called a sibling known 0.3 percent of the time at spread 12 (A 48.6, E 47.8) and 17 percent at spread 6 (A 40, E 58); by p=0.15 it has fallen back to the others (27 to 46 percent). So the noise estimate, which added little on random patterns (previous row), is what separates 'the same' from 'similar' when the noise is low. When the sibling distance is about the same as the noise, no strategy can tell them apart. Implication for the reply: a yes/no 'familiar' hides this; the reply should carry the degree of similarity (best score against the noise estimate: near-exact, or close but not the same). Not modelled: that graded reply itself; the real block's counters. |
| GRADED REPLY WITH A LEAP FROM THE FAMILY, TRAINED BY THE REAL OUTCOME, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_grade_v1.py, imports hexn_noise_fb_v2.py; 3 seeds, 6,000 queries per stream; model only, nothing on a board; plain compare-all store, not the real block) | 500 stored 64-wire patterns in 25 families (spread 12 or 6, as the similar-patterns row). Each pattern has one of 8 OUTCOMES: its family's outcome with probability 0.8, otherwise random; a never-stored sibling gets its outcome by the same rule. THIS FAMILY STRUCTURE OF THE OUTCOMES IS AN ASSUMPTION BUILT INTO THE DATA, not measured; the ceiling for a family guess is therefore 0.8. Queries: 1/3 known, 1/3 sibling, 1/3 random (a random pattern has no outcome, so any answer to it is wrong). BIN = today: one look, fixed threshold 0.75, answer the best match's outcome or nothing. GRADE = two looks (third if noisy), disagreeing wires unknown, noise estimate; EXACT = best match above the noise-based and chance-level thresholds and strictly ahead (answer its outcome); CLOSE = not exact but several stored patterns score above the chance level (answer by a vote of them weighted by score - 0.5: the leap); UNKNOWN otherwise. Training feedback: after each answer the true outcome arrives and the system keeps hits and tries per (grade, score band), giving a learned confidence (hits+1)/(tries+2). No stored pattern is added or changed in this model. | Share of ALL queries answered correctly: spread 6: GRADE 0.585 to 0.600 against BIN 0.401 to 0.431 (all three noise levels); spread 12: GRADE 0.416 to 0.462 against BIN 0.376 to 0.443 (small gain). Sibling queries, spread 6: GRADE answered 0.997 to 1.000 of them, right 0.755 to 0.800 of the answers (near the 0.8 ceiling), against BIN answering 0.417 to 0.435, right 0.687 to 0.697. Sibling queries, spread 12: GRADE answered 0.355 to 0.559, right 0.69 to 0.70 (BIN 0.22 to 0.48 answered). The grades mean something: accuracy EXACT 0.897 to 0.998 against CLOSE 0.660 to 0.838; the LEARNED confidence tracks the actual accuracy per grade within about 0.03 (EXACT 0.998 against 0.998, CLOSE 0.660 against 0.695 at spread 12, p=0.03; the aggregate difference mean confidence minus actual is between -0.009 and +0.012). Answers with learned confidence at least 0.7 are right 0.905 to 0.963 of the time and cover 0.38 to 0.62 of all queries. Limits: (a) at spread 12 the CLOSE vote seldom fires because a sibling scores below the chance-level floor (siblings are about 21 wires from a member); that floor is meant for a single best match and is too strict for a vote over many members, so a lower floor for the vote is the next thing to test, with the risk of answering random patterns; (b) the benefit exists only if the real outcomes do share a family value; with outcomes unrelated across a family the leap would be wrong 7 times in 8 here, and the learned confidence should then fall, which this run does not test; (c) the sibling called EXACT at high noise is still counted wrong when its outcome differs; (d) training here is only confidence; adding or updating stored patterns from the outcome is not modelled. |
| GRADED REPLY: OUTCOMES NOT SHARED ACROSS A FAMILY, AND LOWERING THE CONFIDENCE LEVEL, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_grade_v2.py, clone of v1 which is left untouched; noise p=0.08, 3 seeds, 6,000 queries per stream, second half scored; model only, nothing on a board; plain compare-all store) | Q = chance that a pattern's outcome equals its family's: 0.8 (as the previous row), 0.5, and 0.125 (8 outcomes, so NO sharing). VZ = looseness of the floor under the CLOSE vote (4.2 as before, 2.0 looser). The reply is accepted only if its learned confidence is at least a LEVEL (0.9, 0.7, 0.5, 0.3, 0). Columns: share of all queries answered, accuracy of those, share of RANDOM queries answered (always wrong). | (1) The learned confidence is calibrated: the mean confidence of the accepted answers is within 0.01 of their accuracy in nearly every row (for example 0.873 against 0.880, 0.728 against 0.728, 0.589 against 0.592). (2) NO SHARING (Q=0.125, strict floor): the family leap is not trusted. At any level from 0.3 up the system answers only the known third of the queries, 0.998 to 1.000 right, and abstains on siblings; only at level 0 does it answer them (spread 6: 0.668 answered, 0.582 right, the siblings alone about 0.17 right, i.e. chance level). So the confidence does protect against a false intuition. (3) PARTIAL SHARING (Q=0.5): level 0.7 admits only exact answers (0.331 answered, 0.999 right); lowering to 0.3 admits the leaps (0.667 answered, 0.728 right), a coverage doubling for 27 points of accuracy; at spread 12 the same step gives 0.467 at 0.815. (4) STRONG SHARING (Q=0.8, spread 6): lowering the level from 0.7 to 0.5 adds coverage (0.540 to 0.668 answered, 0.920 to 0.880 right); below 0.5 nothing more is added (the confidences of the leaps are all above 0.5). (5) THE LOOSER VOTE FLOOR is not safe as it stands: at spread 6 it gives no gain; at spread 12 and level 0.5 it lifts coverage from 0.469 to 0.710 but 15 percent of RANDOM queries are then answered (0.151, strict 0.005) and accuracy falls 0.906 to 0.833; at level 0.7, 3.3 percent random answered; at level 0 it answers 97 to 100 percent of random queries. Cause: the confidence is learned only per (grade, score band), so true siblings and random patterns that fall in the same band share one confidence and the random ones are carried along. A band that also uses how many members support the vote, or how strongly they agree, is the next thing to test. Reading: lower the confidence level only as far as the data's own sharing earns it; the level is the safety, and the vote floor and level must be tuned together. |
| GRADED REPLY: VOTE SUPPORT AND AGREEMENT IN THE CONFIDENCE BAND, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_grade_v3.py, clone of v2 which is left untouched; noise p=0.08, 3 seeds, 6,000 queries per stream, second half scored; loose vote floor VZ=2.0; model only, nothing on a board; plain compare-all store) | Tests the fix proposed in the previous row. For a CLOSE answer the confidence band now also holds SUPPORT (how many stored patterns score above the vote floor: under 3, 3 to 8, 9 or more) and AGREEMENT (the winning outcome's share of the vote weight: under 0.5, 0.5 to 0.8, 0.8 or more). 'plain' = the previous band. | PARTIAL fix. Spread 12 (siblings about 21 wires from a member), Q=0.8: at level 0.7 the new band answers 0.699 of sibling queries against 0.293, total answered 0.578 against 0.442, with random queries answered 0.035 against 0.033 (accuracy 0.898 against 0.931). At level 0.5 and 0.3 it does NOT remove the leak: random queries answered 0.131 and 0.140 (plain 0.151). Spread 12, Q=0.5: at level 0.5 random answered 0.009 against 0.132, siblings 0.209 against 0.020, accuracy 0.922 (a clear gain), but at level 0.3 random answered is still 0.118. No sharing (Q=0.125): both bands stay safe (random answered at most 0.002, accuracy at least 0.998 for spread 12). Spread 6: no change in either direction (the strict floor already answered 0.997 of siblings with almost no random leakage; the loose floor is not needed there). Likely reason the leak survives at low levels: at a loose floor a random pattern has about 11 stored patterns above the floor by chance, so its support looks like a sibling's. Reading: for loosely related families, the loose floor plus the support-and-agreement band at level 0.7 buys about 14 points of coverage (0.442 to 0.578) for about 3 points of accuracy and 3.5 percent of random patterns answered; whether that is worth it depends on the cost of a false answer, which is not modelled. The strict floor (previous rows) remains the safe default. |
| OUTCOME FEEDBACK CHANGES THE STORED PATTERNS, PYTHON MODEL (24 Oct; tools/hexn_learn_v1.py, imports hexn_noise_fb_v2.py; 3 seeds, 8,000 queries in windows of 1,000, p=0.08; model only, nothing on a board; plain compare-all store with a size limit, NOT the real block) | World: 25 families (spread 6 or 12), a pool of 600 objects each with an outcome (family value with probability Q=0.8, or Q=0.125 = no sharing), queries drawn uniformly from the pool (objects recur about 11 times each) plus 1/6 random patterns with no true outcome and no feedback. The reply is the graded reply of the previous rows (strict vote floor), accepted if learned confidence >= 0.5. ASSUMED: the true outcome arrives after every pool query, whether or not the system answered. Store limit 400 slots (least recently used leaves), start with 100 pool objects. STATIC never stores. LEARN_A stores the pattern (majority of the 3 looks, about 1.8 percent of its wires wrong at p=0.08) when no answer was accepted or the accepted answer was wrong. LEARN_B also stores when a CLOSE answer was right. | Correct / wrong share of pool queries, window 1 to window 8. Spread 6, Q=0.8: STATIC 0.75 / 0.20 throughout; LEARN_A 0.83 / 0.15 rising to 0.98 / 0.02 by window 5 (store 397 of 400); LEARN_B 0.93 / 0.07 (store full from window 2, so it evicts). Spread 6, Q=0.125 (no sharing): STATIC 0.16 / 0.00; LEARN_A 0.48 rising to 0.68 correct, 0.05 wrong (both modes the same), which is the capacity ceiling 400/600 = 0.67. Spread 12, Q=0.8: STATIC 0.23 / 0.03; LEARN_A 0.58 rising to 0.75 / 0.03; LEARN_B 0.73 / 0.03. Spread 12, Q=0.125: STATIC 0.17; LEARN_A 0.66 / 0.00. Random patterns answered: 0.00 in every window and mode (strict floor). Reading: (1) feeding the outcome back into the store takes the strong-sharing tight-family case from 0.76 correct / 0.20 wrong to 0.98 / 0.02 with 400 slots for 600 objects, because the stored members plus the family leap cover the objects that did not fit; (2) storing only what the system did not know or got wrong (A) is better than also storing right-but-close answers (B) when memory is limited, because B fills the store and evicts useful members (0.93 against 0.98); (3) with no sharing the system simply memorises, and what it knows is limited by capacity (0.68 of a pool that is 1.5 times the store); the 5 percent wrong at spread 6 comes from stored near-twins (siblings 11 wires apart whose outcomes differ) being matched exactly through noise. NOT MODELLED: outcomes that change (an update to a stored pattern's outcome), objects that stop recurring, a drifting world, the real block's 8 slots and counters (the hashed RAM tier would hold this store), a feedback delay or feedback that arrives only sometimes, and a bigger pool than the store at a larger scale. |
| BRINGING THE PIECES INTO ONE CARD MODEL, STEP 1: THE BRIDGE ON USER2 (24 Oct; Alan: inference, feedback learning, the odd looks and the RAM modelling are done in Python, what is left is a wider protocol and the second JTAG user; files: fpga/ypcb/hexn_u2/ (clone of fpga/ypcb/hexn: module hexn_u2, bridge CHAIN 2, check.py sets HEXN_CHAIN=2), tools/hexn_jtag_host_v1.py (additive: OpenOCDExec ir defaults from env HEXN_CHAIN, 1 = IR 0x02 as before, 2 = 0x03, 3 = 0x22, 4 = 0x23); the proven folder fpga/ypcb/hexn and the bridge rtl/hexn_jtag_bridge_v1.v are untouched) | The bridge already had the chain as a parameter (CHAIN, passed to BSCANE2 JTAG_CHAIN), so no RTL changed. The ypcb DDR3 debug register stays on USER1 and Alan's ypcb repo is not touched; in a joint design Hex-N is the one that moves. Checks done in the sandbox: hexn_u2 plus the hex7 core elaborates (iverilog), the existing link checks still pass after the host change (tools/hexn_jtag_check_v1.py JTAG BRIDGE SIM PASS, tools/hexn_jtag_sessions_check_v1.py TWO-SESSION TEST PASS). The simulation model of BSCANE2 does not look at the chain number, so the chain itself can only be tested on the card. | NOT YET ON THE CARD: build hexn_u2 (a hex7, 7-cell build is the quick one), load it, run a test with HEXN_CHAIN=2; the same test with HEXN_CHAIN=1 must then fail to get an answer (the proof that the bridge really is on USER2). Then the same on a build that also holds the ypcb DDR3 debug register. |
| PROTOCOL V2, DESIGN ONLY, NOTHING BUILT (24 Oct; read from rtl/hexn_core_v2.v and rtl/hexn_jtag_bridge_v1.v) | Facts from the RTL: the bridge carries 14 data bytes per 160-bit frame with byte counters modulo 256 and a resend scheme, so a long reply (RUNCELLN is 2 x NC + K bytes, 529 bytes at 255 cells) is just more frames; the bridge needs no change. The limits are in the core: the cell number in LOADCELLN (0x11) and RUNCELLN (0x12) is one byte (NC at most 255); a cell's lane settings are 5 bytes, 2 bits per detector (K at most 20); the reply counter ci is 12 bits in core v2 (NC at most about 2,000 at K=19). PROPOSAL, additive: all existing opcodes keep their meaning, so every proven test runs unchanged; new opcodes only. 0x13 LOADCELLW [cell high][cell low][threshold][lane bytes, LB = ceil(2K/8) of them] (LB = 5 at K=19, 10 at K=40); 0x14 RUNCELLW (as RUNCELLN, reply layout unchanged, 16-bit counters); 0x30 VERSION, reply [protocol 2][NC high][NC low][K][wires][slots][flags: bit 0 = RAM present], so a host can read what the build holds instead of being told; 0x31 RAMRD [address, 4 bytes, 25 bits used], reply the 64 data bytes of one DDR3 word; 0x32 RAMWR [address, 4 bytes][64 bytes], acknowledged 0xA5 when the controller has taken it; 0x33 RAMSTAT (calibration done, controller state, error flags); later opcodes from 0x34 for the hashed tier in hardware (save and recall through the tables) once the host-driven version has run. COST: LOADCELLW with 16-bit cell numbers indexes the settings arrays by a wider number (more decode logic); the arrays themselves grow with NC as now (about 285 LUT sites per cell placed). Host side: the cell tests take the new opcodes from a v2 host module (a clone, not an edit of the proven ones). OPEN: how many cells the card should hold (by LUT count, thousands; by clock, the placed clock already fell from 74 MHz at 128 cells to 60 to 66 MHz at 255), whether more than 20 detectors is wanted at all before a bigger lattice exists, and where the memory block sits relative to the cells in the joint design. |
| BRIDGE ON USER2, ON THE CARD (24 Oct; fpga/ypcb/hexn_u2, hex7 shape, 7 cells, core v2; build_u2_hex7; Alan ran the commands) | build: synth 98 s, pnr 1567 s, frames 76 s; nextpnr br.clk 82.01 / 92.35 MHz (PASS at 50), br.drck 603.50 / 654.02 MHz; bitstream hexn_u2.bit 18,735,111 bytes. Loaded with openFPGALoader (digilent_hs2, 6 MHz), then tools/hexn_card_bist_v1.py --cells 7 --wires 18 --aw 7 with HEXN_CHAIN=2. | BIST PASS: all 8 sets, every stage ok (126 counters, 126 weights, 7 sums, 7 thresholds, 28 answers and bursts per set) and 24 of 24 divider tests. So the Hex-N bridge answers on USER2 (IR 0x03) and the host change works. The same loaded design with HEXN_CHAIN=1 (IR 0x02) got no answer: Alan reported it 'stuck' and stopped it (his words; no output was kept), which is the expected result for a bridge that is not on USER1, but a hang is a weaker proof than a clean 'no answer' message. NOT YET DONE: a build that holds both the Hex-N bridge (USER2) and the ypcb DDR3 debug register (USER1) in one bitstream. |
| JOINT DESIGN: DDR3 PROBE AND HEX-N IN ONE BITSTREAM, PREPARED (24 Oct; fpga/ypcb/hexn_joint/; Alan will build it in the afternoon) | Approach: the generated uberddr3 top level (not visible from the sandbox) is used unchanged; the Hex-N core and its bridge (CHAIN 2 = USER2) sit INSIDE the replacement memtest.v, which is the only hook into that top level. prebuild.py runs ypcb's own uberddr3 prebuild first, then writes gen_01_hexn.v with the Hex-N RTL (hex7, 7 cells by default). The ypcb debug register keeps USER1. Checked in the sandbox: the Hex-N half of prebuild (with a stand-in for ypcb's prebuild) and iverilog elaboration of the joint memtest. | NOT built, NOT run. Known risk: the core runs on the DDR3 controller clock (83.33 MHz), near the 82.0 / 92.4 MHz nextpnr estimate of the stand-alone hex7 build, and the flow's timing check only tests 50 MHz; a flaky BIST with a passing DDR3 check would point here. Not known to me: the exact build command and settings Alan used for hexn_ram (the README says to reuse them). Test order after the load: BIST on USER2, then ypcb check.py and memlat.py on USER1. |
| DIRECTION, NOT A RESULT: TWO STAGES, CLEAN THEN WORK (Alan, 24 Oct 2026, after the inference, noise-feedback, learning and pair models of this day) | Stage 1 (the counted detector bank, proven on the Tang and the Kintex card) cleans the incoming data and turns it into even, tidy patterns (tokens). Stage 2 is the workhorse: it works on the clean tokens, storing, sifting and being trained (the graded reply, the family leap, learning from outcomes, the memory block and the card RAM tier of today's models). This extends the 22 Oct direction (bank as cleaning front end for the full cell) and fits the pair model (two noise-tolerant front ends into one exact table: 89,600 bits against 512,000 in the model). Why it helps, from the models: stage 2 can match exactly (no noise side), its size is counted in tokens not wires, and the stages are trained separately, which avoids the credit-assignment failure of searching both stages together (hexn_comb_v1: 0.475 against 0.627 for the bank alone). | OPEN, to be decided before building: what a token is (the winner's id only, which allows an exact table, or the K burst counts 0 to 7 per detector, which keep graded confidence but are not exactly repeatable under noise, so stage 2 would still need a similarity match, only over a much cleaner vector, or the winner plus a confidence band); what happens when stage 1 is unsure (pass 'unknown', or pass its confidence on); and whether stage 2 sits in the card fabric, in the host or in the RAM tier first. Not tested yet: a model with the trained detector bank as stage 1 and the graded-reply memory with learning as stage 2. |

## WHAT IS PROVEN WHERE (24 Oct 2026; Alan's way of working: every stage is tested as a part of its own before it is trusted in the whole; update this table whenever a row changes, never edit a result silently)

'yes' = done and passed, with the figure in the notes; 'model' = Python model only; '-' = not done. Pass criterion for every board row: the board's answers equal the Python model's, wire for wire.

| Piece | Python model | Simulation | Tang Nano 20K | Kintex card | Notes (figures from the rows above) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Detector bank, 7 and 19 detectors, 18 wires | yes | yes | yes | yes | simulation: byte-level identical on 350 of 350 and 950 of 950 samples; Tang: identical on 5,250 of 5,250 and 14,250 of 14,250; Kintex: BIST PASS at 7 and 19 detectors; real silicon clock of the Kintex builds not measured |

| TWO-STAGE MODEL: DETECTOR BANK AS STAGE 1, A LEARNING MEMORY AS STAGE 2, FOUR TOKEN FORMS (24 Oct; tools/hexn_twostage_v1.py, imports the board-verified bank model tools/hexn_bank_model_v2.py; 3 seeds, 4,000 events in windows of 500; model only, nothing on a board; stage 2 is a Python stand-in) | Two sensors, each seen through its own trained 7-class detector bank (18 wires, test noise q per wire). An event is a pair of true classes (49 pairs, uniform); its outcome (one of 8) comes from a fixed random table over the pairs, so there is NO sharing and nothing can be guessed: it must be learned. The true outcome arrives after every event (ASSUMED); stage 2 stores the key with the true outcome when it did not answer or was wrong. No family leap: this tests cleaning, learning speed and size, not generalisation. Token forms: T1 the two winner ids (a tie or silence on either sensor = no answer), an exact table; T2 winner ids plus a confidence band per sensor (top count minus second >= 3); T3 the two vectors of 7 burst counts, nearest stored vector by sum of absolute differences, accepted at 8 or less; B0 (no stage 1) the raw 36 wires of both sensors, nearest stored pattern by Hamming distance, accepted at 12 or less. Acceptance limits were fixed once, NOT tuned per noise level. | Stage 1 gets both classes right in 0.908 / 0.686 / 0.316 of events at noise 0.05 / 0.15 / 0.25, with a tie or silence on either sensor in 0.087 / 0.256 / 0.536. Last window (correct / wrong / no answer), noise 0.05: T1 0.90 / 0.01 / 0.09 (49 entries, 441 bits), T2 0.90 / 0.01 / 0.09 (80 entries, 876 bits), T3 0.95 / 0.05 / 0.00 (225 entries, 10,140 bits), B0 0.98 / 0.02 / 0.00 (125 entries, 4,888 bits). Noise 0.15: T1 0.65 / 0.11 / 0.24 (49 entries, 441 bits), T2 0.65 / 0.10 / 0.25 (110, 1,214), T3 0.64 / 0.35 / 0.00 (1,313 entries, 59,100 bits), B0 0.74 / 0.26 / 0.00 (1,243 entries, 48,464 bits). Noise 0.25: T1 0.24 / 0.23 / 0.53, T2 0.25 / 0.22 / 0.54 (both small), T3 0.31 / 0.69 / 0.00 (2,722 entries, 122,505 bits), B0 0.38 / 0.62 / 0.00 (2,644 entries, 103,103 bits). Reading: (1) the winner-id table (T1) is the stage-2 form to start with: it learns in about two windows, its size stays at the 49 pairs (441 bits) whatever the noise, while T3 and B0 keep adding an entry for every noisy variant they meet (100 times more bits at noise 0.15); (2) it pays for that with no-answer events: a tie or silence in stage 1 loses the whole pair (0.24 of events at noise 0.15), and its share of right answers among the answers given is 0.86 at noise 0.15 against 0.74 (B0) and 0.65 (T3); (3) the confidence band (T2) adds nothing here, and the burst-count vector (T3) is worse than the winner alone under this fixed limit; (4) at low noise the single raw-wire model (B0) is the most accurate (0.98) but at noise 0.15 it is wrong in 0.26 of events. Limits: world with exact repeated pairs from a small vocabulary (7 classes per sensor), which favours the staged form; limits not tuned (a tuned B0 or T3 may do better); true outcome always arrives; no family leap. NEXT: reduce stage 1's ties by giving it more than one look; the leaky cell totals carried between windows (proven on the card) are the hardware way. |
| TWO-STAGE MODEL WITH MORE THAN ONE LOOK IN STAGE 1 (24 Oct; tools/hexn_twostage_v2.py, a new file beside v1 which is left untouched; 3 seeds, 4,000 events, the last 1,000 averaged; model only, nothing on a board) | Same world as the previous row (two sensors, 49 pairs, 8 outcomes with no sharing, true outcome always arrives, limits fixed and not tuned). Stage 1 takes L independent looks at each sensor and SUMS each class's burst count over the looks (what the leaky cell totals do when carried between windows with no leak); winner = the unique highest total, a tie or all-zero = no answer. Stage 2 = the winner-id table (T1). Reference B0 = raw wires, each wire the majority of L looks (for L=2 the first look is used, so L=2 is the same as L=1 for B0), nearest stored pattern accepted at 12 of 36 or less. Independent noise in every look is ASSUMED. | Noise 0.15, L = 1 / 2 / 3 / 5: stage 1 gets both classes right 0.686 / 0.883 / 0.958 / 0.990; T1 correct / wrong / no answer 0.65 / 0.10 / 0.24, 0.87 / 0.05 / 0.08, 0.95 / 0.02 / 0.02, 0.99 / 0.01 / 0.00 (right among answers 0.86 / 0.94 / 0.97 / 0.99), always 441 bits; B0 0.72 / 0.28 / 0.00, 0.71 / 0.29, 0.97 / 0.03 with 7,397 bits, 1.00 / 0.00 with 2,405 bits (B0 at L=1 and 2: 48,464 and 50,154 bits). Noise 0.25, L = 1 / 2 / 3 / 5: stage 1 both right 0.316 / 0.531 / 0.682 / 0.818; T1 0.25 / 0.22 / 0.53, 0.45 / 0.25 / 0.31, 0.63 / 0.20 / 0.18, 0.75 / 0.16 / 0.09 (right among answers 0.53 / 0.65 / 0.76 / 0.83), 441 bits; B0 0.37 / 0.63, 0.36 / 0.64, 0.70 / 0.30 (52,858 bits), 0.87 / 0.13 (22,789 bits). Reading: (1) more looks is what matters most: at noise 0.15 three looks take the winner-id table from 0.65 correct / 0.24 no answer to 0.95 / 0.02 and five looks to 0.99; (2) two looks help the summed-count stage 1 (0.65 to 0.87) because the sum breaks most ties, while an even number of looks gives the majority-vote reference nothing; (3) the raw-wire model with majority cleaning is as accurate as the staged form at noise 0.15 (0.97 against 0.95 at three looks) and MORE accurate at noise 0.25 (0.87 against 0.75 at five looks), so the staged form is not an accuracy win in this world; its advantage is size, a flat 441 bits against 2,405 to 7,397 bits at the same accuracy (5 to 17 times), and that stage 2 never sees raw wires; (4) the raw model's cleaning is itself a front end (a majority per wire), so what is compared is two kinds of cleaning, one on the wires and one through the detector bank. Not modelled: noise that repeats from look to look, the cost of the extra looks in time (L windows per answer), and generalisation (no family leap). |

Findings: (1) a gap of exactly one cycle is the worst, as expected (identical hum, nothing cleared); (2) **my earlier prediction that a half-cycle gap would cancel best was wrong in this model: a quarter-cycle gap is best, half-cycle is in between**; I have not worked out why and do not claim a mechanism; (3) strong hum costs up to 13 points at L=3 even with the best spacing tried (0.934 vs 0.954 independent), weak hum costs 0-3 points; (4) 50 Hz and 100 Hz rows are identical once the gap is expressed in cycles, so the rule is in cycles, not milliseconds. Next: real hum amplitude and coupling come from the ESP logger CSVs. |

| 26 Oct 2026 | LINE OF CELLS, FIRST GATE (Alan: the result should pass from cell to cell, connectivity and feedback are the key; `tools/hexn_net_v1.py`, 200 random lines per length, Python model only, nothing on a board) | **Model only.** Cell = the v2 model (`hexn_cell_v2_model.py`, all faces feed one decision). ASSUMPTION, not checked here: the RTL cell v5 used in the bank behaves as that model (the v2 model header says 'no RTL yet'). Line: cell i face 0 <-> cell i+1 face 3, both directions, same clock; cell 0 driven from outside; weak input (0.03 per lane per clock) against strong (0.15); 'passes information' = the last cell's output (clocks high over the last 4 windows) differs between the two. Random cells from a signal-passing family (gate codes AND/CHOOSE/STEER only, NO negative lanes). **Thresholds 1..12:** passes 0.99 / 0.77 / 0.56 / 0.39 / 0.19 / 0.10 at K = 1 / 2 / 3 / 4 / 6 / 8; stuck at zero 0.01 / 0.22 / 0.42 / 0.59 / 0.80 / 0.90 (the signal dies). **Thresholds 1..4:** passes 0.99 / 0.93 / 0.84 / 0.74 / 0.64 / 0.58; stuck equal and non-zero 0.01 / 0.06 / 0.14 / 0.20 / 0.24 / 0.26 (the signal saturates); mean output weak/strong 32.7/63.4 at K = 1 but 28.7/45.2 at K = 8, so the dynamic range is squeezed. Stronger input never gave LESS output (no case in 2,400 lines). **Reading:** a random line fades or saturates with length, and the thresholds decide which; keeping a graded signal alive over many cells needs the settings to be found, which is what the feedback learning must do. **Limits:** this measures only input STRENGTH reaching the end, not which pattern; no negative (inhibitory) lanes in the family; one pair of input strengths; no learning yet. Because a v2 cell adds every lane into ONE total, a chain carries one number; telling patterns apart needs several cells side by side with different lane settings (the model header says the same), so the next step is a layer of cells, not a longer line. |

| 26 Oct 2026 | DESIGN DECISION (Alan's, 26 Oct; an INTENTION, nothing built, nothing modelled yet): the ENGRAM IS TWO-TIER | Chain: detector layer cleans the input into even patterns -> layers of cells -> teaching sets pair input patterns with goals -> the cells' feedback finds the best path -> the path is stored as an engram -> the recall and match system uses it. **Two forms of engram.** (1) QUICK recall: the ACTIVATION PATTERN (which cells fired and how strongly) is a short bit pattern kept in the existing memory block (`hexn_mem_v1`, 8 slots, matching over the known wires); it is the immediate, cheap recall. (2) DEEP recall: the SETTINGS the learning found (lane off/add/subtract and thresholds of every cell on the path); the quick pattern is the KEY that is matched and then recalls the deep one. This is the same fast tier / RAM tier split as the tiers model (`hexn_tiers_v2.py`, `v3.py`: slots buy speed, RAM size buys capacity): the key sits in the fast tier, the settings blob in the DDR3 tier (protocol v2 RAMRD/RAMWR, 64-byte words, design only). **Size estimate (my arithmetic on the model's encoding, not measured):** a 6-cell hidden layer (20 ternary lanes = 40 bits + threshold about 5 bits per cell) plus 3 output cells (6 lanes = 12 bits + threshold 4 bits) is about 318 bits, so one network's settings fit in ONE 64-byte (512-bit) DDR3 word. **Not yet shown:** that a stored activation pattern is distinctive enough to act as a key; that loading recalled settings back reproduces the behaviour; any of this in RTL. Next in the model: the two-layer learning runs (in progress), then store (key, settings), recall by key, reload and check the behaviour matches. |

| 26 Oct 2026 | TWO-LAYER NETWORK: HAND-WIRED, THEN FEEDBACK LEARNING (`tools/hexn_layer_v1.py`, `tools/hexn_layer_learn_v1.py`; Python model only; v2 cell model, assumption that RTL cell v5 behaves like it NOT checked; 3 random 20-bit patterns, 6 hidden cells + 3 output cells, input pulsed for 2 clocks, 4 windows per presentation) | **Model only.** (a) Hand-wired network (hidden cell = template of one pattern, output cell = copy of one hidden cell), 5 seeds, test right/wrong/none: noise 0.1 0.88/0.00/0.12; noise 0.2 0.52/0.00/0.48. So the cells CAN express the task. Mutual inhibition in the output layer changed nothing (15 settings, identical to 2 decimals), which fits the winner already being the largest burst; observed, not proved. (b) Feedback learning, 5 seeds, 1,500 tries, keep-if-not-worse, learner sees only the teacher's reward for noisy batches (trained at noise 0.1). **GRADED reward** (right output minus strongest other, plus right/wrong/none terms): LEARN 0.52/0.11/0.36 at noise 0.1 and 0.48/0.19/0.33 at 0.2; RANDOM (best of the same number of random networks) 0.38/0.07/0.55 and 0.31/0.13/0.55; HAND 0.88 and 0.52 right. Per-seed right at noise 0.1: 0.62 0.33 0.67 0.33 0.67 (large spread). So learning beats random guessing, is far below the hand-wired network at low noise (0.52 vs 0.88) and about level with it at noise 0.2 (0.48 vs 0.52, with more wrong answers); the learned networks lose less when the noise rises. **A flaw found on the way:** with the PLAIN reward (none scores 0, wrong -0.5) the learner settles on a network that never answers (silence is free): 0.00 right in the 1-seed trial; with only 'none costs -0.25' two of five seeds still ended at 0.00 right (0.25 mean right at noise 0.1). The reward must reward the right output ACTIVELY (graded). **Limits:** 5 seeds with large spread; 1,500 tries; one hidden-layer size; patterns random at density 0.5, 3 classes only; the teacher computes the reward from the network's outputs (a real on-chip teacher is not designed); nothing in RTL. Not yet tried: more tries, other hidden sizes, more classes, the stored-engram step (key from the activation pattern, settings recalled by it). |

| 26 Oct 2026 | DESIGN INTENTION (Alan's, 26 Oct; nothing built, nothing modelled): TWO SEPARATE PATHS | (1) The wiring between cells is LOCAL (point to point, the flow of the signal). (2) The stored pattern (the engram's settings) travels on the COMMAND side, not on the signal wiring, so each path stays quiet. Fit with what exists: settings already arrive by command (`0x11`: cell number, threshold, lane bytes, one cell per command) and are held in the core, which is the point flagged in `TILE_SHARED_SIGNALS_v1.md` (47 setting flops per cell in the core, one index decode with the data broadcast to every cell). **My reading, to be checked:** on-chip 'noise' here is mostly congestion and timing (many cells hanging on one net), not electrical noise; keeping signal wiring local and putting settings on a separate command path should help that, but only if the command path is itself local to each tile (a chain through the tiles, settings registers inside the tile); a command bus that still reaches every cell from one point would bring the fan-out back. The sweep (variant A, registered broadcasts) is the only evidence so far that this class of change helps. **Open design items:** how stored settings get from the RAM tier to the cells on the chip (today only the host sends commands; an on-chip sequencer that reads a settings word from DDR3 and issues the loads is not designed); the current lane settings are per detector token (2 bits per token, up to 20), not per physical lane of a layered network, so the command format would need a form for layer-to-layer wiring. |

Rules compared (only the thresholds change; the loader can write any threshold): **midpoint** (the bank's rule, learned weights): 6 of 21 clean codes right; **exact** (threshold at the smallest own-class total, learned weights): 19 of 21; **direct wiring** (weights set straight from each code, +1 for a 1 bit, -1 for a 0 bit, threshold at the code's number of ones): **21 of 21**. **Single-bit flips of the 21 codes (168 cases):** 48 land on ANOTHER VALID key and are read as that key by every rule (a dense code has no redundancy: nothing in the bank can see that as an error; a check bit, as NEC's inverse byte, or context from layer 2 would). The other 120 flips land on unused codes: with direct wiring and the exact threshold all 120 give 'unknown' and so do all 2000 random far codes (the bank refuses what it does not know); with one wire of slack (graded: exact match gives a high output, one wire off gives output 1) the 120 give 54 answers naming the original key, 4 naming another key, 56 ties between two or more keys (the unused code is one bit from several valid keys) and 6 unknown; far codes still 100 percent unknown. **Reading:** the bank CAN tell one-bit-different codes apart, and give a graded reply (exact / close / unknown), if the thresholds are set at each detector's own exact total instead of the midpoint; direct wiring from the code table gives it perfectly, learned weights get most of the way (19 of 21, 77 percent of far codes rejected). Default N=3 W=20 on the same streams: 0.000 / 0.022 / 0.611 / 3.111 per 1000, new routine at block 218 to 222. **So the data-set level cuts false promotions about 16 times at 60% noise, at the cost of the new routine being promoted about 10 to 20 blocks later; at low noise it is looser and quicker.** The real run 3 alone (one anomaly, the extra 4) is too little data, so the host keeps the default. **Limits:** noise injected, not measured; variant frequencies as seen in training (unseen variants not allowed for); blocks compared whole; no RTL for the candidate store or counters. **Front end (Alan, 27 Oct 2026; design intention, nothing built):** a simple front end is needed later for data in and results out; results come from layer 3, with the layer 1 and layer 2 interim steps optionally shown alongside. |

KNOWN (128 entries: the three real runs need 76 pairs, runs 1 and 2 need 70, a clean random-order stream of the four routines produces 74): each entry is (context of 1, 2 or 3 events, next event) with a count seen and a count contradicted. CAND (capacity set by the host): every pair not yet known gets a sighting at all three context lengths; N sightings inside W events (host-set rate rule) promotes it to KNOWN carrying its count; when full the weakest candidate is dropped. Retrieve reads only KNOWN: longest known context, best next event, its share of the count at that context, total count. The self-check verdict (ok / contradiction / novel) is as in the anomaly model. Time is in events (about 4 per block, so the host's W in blocks times 4). **A.
